The problem is hard even if the numerator and denominator of each entry are given in unary.

(A25) Rank-Constrained Optimization

Given:

Two matrices
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times n}
, a natural number
k
.

Question:

Is there an
n\times n
matrix
X
in the positive semidefinite cone
S^{n}_{+}
such that
AX=B
and
\operatorname{rank}(X)\leq k
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Bertsimas, Cory-Wright, and Pauphilet
[
BCWP22
]
via a reduction from
linkage realizability
(CG65) Linkage Realizability
.

Comments:

In Bertsimas et al.
[
BCWP22
]
, the general problem of minimizing an expression of the form
\langle C,X\rangle+\lambda\cdot\operatorname{rank}(X)
subject to
AX=B
,
\operatorname{rank}(X)\leq k
and
X\in\mathcal{K}
for some proper cone
\mathcal{K}
is considered.
This problem is already
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard for
\mathcal{K}=S^{n}_{+}
,
k=2
,
\lambda=1
and
C=\mathbf{0}
by reduction from unit linkage realizability.

Also see:

Linkage realizability
(CG65) Linkage Realizability
.

(A26) Tensor Rank

Given:

A
3
-dimensional tensor
T=(t_{ijk})\in\mathbb{Q}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times d_{3}}
, a natural number
k
.

Question:

Is the
(tensor) rank
of
T
at most
k
; that is, are there at most
k
rank-
1
tensors
T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}
such
that
T=\sum_{i\in[k]}T_{i}
? A tensor has
rank-
1
if it can be written as
u\otimes v\otimes w
for real vectors
u,v,w
, where
\otimes
is the outer (Kronecker) product of two vectors.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ18
]
via a reduction from a restricted version of the minimum matrix rank problem
(A16) Minimum/Maximum Matrix Rank
, and Shitov
[
Shi16a
]
via a reduction from feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
.
The problem remains hard for symmetric tensors
[
Shi16a
]
.

Comments:

Tensor rank was famously shown
\mathsf{NP}
-hard by Håstad
[
Hås90
]
.
Shitov
[
Shi16a
]
showed that over an integral domain, the (symmetric) tensor rank problem is as hard as the existential theory of that domain.
For a related notion, called
border rank
, whose complexity is still open, see
[
BIJL18
]
.

Universality:

There is a universality result
[
SŠ24
]
(which fixes the version in
[
SŠ18
]
).

Open Questions:

Does the problem remain hard for fixed
k
?

Also see:

Minimum matrix rank
(A16) Minimum/Maximum Matrix Rank
.

(A27) Tensor with Eigenvalue
0

Given:

A
3
-dimensional tensor
T=(t_{ijk})\in\mathbb{Q}^{n\times n\times n}
.

Question:

Does
T
have
0
as an
eigenvalue
; that is, is there a non-zero vector
v\in R^{n}
for which
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}t_{i,j,k}v_{i}v_{j}=0
for all
k\in[n]
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Schaefer
[
Sch13
, Corollary 3.10]
, also see Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ18
, Example 2.5]
.

Comments:

There also is a proof by Hillar and Lim
[
HL13
]
, but it appears to have a gap, see the discussion in
[
SŠ18
, Example 2.5]
. The problem is equivalent to the nontrivial homogenous zero problem (
H
2
N
)
(A6) Nontrivial Homogeneous Zero (H2N)
.
We should note that there are other, non-equivalent, definitions of tensor eigenvalues.

Open Questions:

Is the eigenvalue problem
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for symmetric tensors (with eigenvalues in
\mathbb{Q}[d]
)? Hillar and Lam
[
HL13
, Theorem 9.3]
show that this problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

Also see:

Tensor rank
(A26) Tensor Rank
,
H
2
N

(A6) Nontrivial Homogeneous Zero (H2N)
, tensor eigenvector approximation
(A-Open10) Tensor Eigenvector Approximation
, tensor singular value
(A-Open11) Tensor Singular Value
.

(A28) Orbit Closure Containment

Given:

Two
3
-dimensional tensors
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}^{n\times n\times n}
.

Question:

Is the orbit closure of
T
contained in the orbit closure of
T^{\prime}
under the
\mathsf{GL}_{n}\times\mathsf{GL}_{n}\times\mathsf{GL}_{n}
action in
\mathbb{R}
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Bläser, Ikenmeyer, Lysikov, Pandey, and Schreyer
[
BIL+21
]
via a reduction from minimum rank of linear combination of matrices
(A17) Minimum Rank of Linear Combination of Matrices
.

Comments:

The orbit of
T
under
\mathsf{G}_{n}
is the set
\{gT:g\in\mathsf{G}_{n}\}
, and we consider the closure of these orbits in the Zariski topology. We refer to Bläser et al.
[
BIL+21
]
for detailed definitions and motivations.

Also see:

Minimum rank of linear combination of matrices
(A17) Minimum Rank of Linear Combination of Matrices
, tensor rank
(A26) Tensor Rank
, minimum matrix rank
(A16) Minimum/Maximum Matrix Rank
.

15.3
Semialgebraic and Algebraic Sets

For polynomials and their representations, see Section
15
.
An
algebraic set
is the zero-set of a family of polynomials:
S=\{x\in R^{n}:f_{i}(x)=0
for all
i\in[k]\}
and is specified by its defining polynomials,
f_{i}
,
i\in[k]
. A
basic semialgebraic set
is a set of the form
S=\{x:\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:f_{i}(x)=0
for all
i\in[k],g_{i}(x)>0
for all
i\in[m]\}
. A
semialgebraic set
is the finite union of basic semialgebraic sets.
We will see two ways of specifying a semialgebraic set: via a formula
\varphi
as

S=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\varphi(x)\}
, where
\varphi
is a quantifier-free formula in the theory of the reals with free variables
x
; and via an arithmetic circuit
C
, where
S=\{x:\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:C(x)=1\}
, where
x
are the variables of the circuit
C
. Unless we say otherwise, we assume a semialgebraic set is specified using the formula
\varphi
.

(A29) Cardinality of Semialgebraic Set

Given:

A semialgebraic set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, a natural number
k\geq 1
or
k=\infty
.

Question:

Does
S
contain fewer than
k
elements?

Complexity:

\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete by Cucker and Rosselló
[
CR92
]
via a reduction from feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
for any fixed
k
, including
k=\infty
.

Comments:

Special (named) cases are
k=1
, the
emptiness
and
k=\infty
, the
finiteness
problem. Cucker and Rosselló
[
CR92
]
work in the BSS-model, but their proof carries over to
\forall\mathbb{R}
. If
k
is not fixed, the problem is complete for the counting version of
\exists\mathbb{R}
, see
[
BC06
, Proposition 8.5(ii)]
.

Also see:

Dimension of semialgebraic set
(A30) Dimension of Semialgebraic Set
, feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
.

(A30) Dimension of Semialgebraic Set

Given:

A semialgebraic set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, an integer
d\geq 0
.

Question:

Does
S
have (real) dimension at least
d
; that is, is there a projection
\pi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
(dropping
n-d
coordinates), such that
\pi(S)
contains a non-empty interior?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Koiran
[
Koi99
]
via reduction from feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
, even for any fixed
d\geq 0
.

Comments:

For
d=0
the problem degenerates to the non-emptiness problem of a semialgebraic set
(A29) Cardinality of Semialgebraic Set
.
Cucker and Rosselló
[
CR92
]
looked at the cases
d=n-1
, which they call the hypersurface problem, and
d=n
, which they call the NEI (nonempty interior) problem. They showed that both problems can be described using the exotic quantifier
\exists^{*}
(not formally introduced until Koiran’s papers
[
Koi99
,
Koi00
]
).

The
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hardness reduction is straight-forward, the non-trivial part is showing that the problem lies in
\exists\mathbb{R}
. Koiran
[
Koi99
]
first shows this for the BSS-model, but also explains how to translate the result into the discrete setting, corresponding to
\exists\mathbb{R}
. Koiran had earlier shown that the
complex
dimension of a semialgebraic set is
\exists\mathbb{C}
-complete (again for the BSS-model)
[
Koi97b
]
. For his result Koiran introduces the exotic quantifier
\exists^{*}
and shows how to eliminate it.

Also see:

Local dimension of semialgebraic set
(A31) Local Dimension of Semialgebraic Set
, feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
.

(A31) Local Dimension of Semialgebraic Set

Given:

A semialgebraic set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
x\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}
, an integer
d\geq 0
.

Question:

Does
S
have local dimension at least
d
at
x
; that is, is there a sufficiently small neighborhood
U
of
x
such that the dimension of
S\cap U
is at least
d
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Bürgisser and Cucker
[
BC09
, Corollary 6.6,Corollary 9.4]
even for
d=1
via a reduction from testing whether
0
lies in the closure of a semialgebraic set
(A37) Zero in Closure of Semialgebraic Set
.

Comments:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-membership uses the exotic quantifier
H
, which can be eliminated.

Also see:

Dimension of semialgebraic set
(A30) Dimension of Semialgebraic Set
.

(A32) Convexity of Semialgebraic Set

Given:

A semialgebraic set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, a natural number
n\geq 2
.

Question:

Is
S

convex
, that is, does the line segment
st
belong to
S
for every two points
s,t\in S
?

Complexity:

\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete by Cucker and Rosselló
[
CR92
]
. Also, see Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
.

Comments:

The proof by Cucker and Rosselló
[
CR92
]
is phrased in the BSS-model, but translates easily to the
\forall\mathbb{R}
-completeness setting, as shown in
[
SŠ23
]
.

Also see:

Star-shapedness of semialgebraic set
(A-Open4) Star-Shapedness of Semialgebraic Set
, convexity through translation
(CG-Open31) Convexity Through Translation
.

(A33) Unboundedness of Semialgebraic Set

Given:

A semialgebraic set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Question:

Is
S

unbounded
, that is, is it true that for every
r>0
there is an
x\in S
with
\lVert x\rVert>r
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Cucker, Rosselló
[
CR92
]
, also see
Bürgisser, Cucker
[
BC09
, Proposition 6.4,Corollary 9.4]
, and Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
.

Comments:

Membership in
\exists\mathbb{R}
is the non-trivial part here, since the definition uses a universal quantifier. Cucker, Rosselló
[
CR92
]
work in the BSS-model, but consider a restricted variant which corresponds to
\exists\mathbb{R}
, and obtain membership using an upper bound from real algebraic geometry. Bürgisser and Cucker
[
BC09
]
work with an exotic quantifier and show how to eliminate it; Schaefer and Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
give a reduction from feasibility over a bounded domain.

(A34) Radius of Semialgebraic Set

Given:

A semialgebraic set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
d\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.

Question:

Does
S
have radius at most
d
; that is, is there a point
c\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\lVert c-s\rVert\leq d
for every
s\in S
?

Complexity:

\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete by Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
via reduction from Feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
over a bounded domain.

Comments:

The
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hardness reduction is straight-forward,
the difficulty lies in showing that the problem lies in
\forall\mathbb{R}
by eliminating the quantification over the center.

Also see:

Diameter of semialgebraic set
(A35) Diameter of Semialgebraic Set
.

(A35) Diameter of Semialgebraic Set

Given:

A semialgebraic set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
d\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.

Question:

Does
S
have diameter at most
d
; that is, do every two points in
S
have distance at most
d
from each other?

Complexity:

\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete by Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
via reduction from Feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
over a bounded domain.

Comments:

The strict version, in which we require the diameter to be strictly less than
d
is also
\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete; the only issue is
\forall\mathbb{R}
-membership, but that can be handled using the exotic
H
-quantifier as we saw in Section
2.4
.

Also see:

Radius of semialgebraic set
(A34) Radius of Semialgebraic Set
.

(A36) Density of Semialgebraic Set

Given:

A semialgebraic set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Question:

Is
S
dense in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
? A set
S\subseteq T
is
dense
in
T
if for every
t\in T
and every
\varepsilon>0
there is an
s\in S
such that
\lVert s-t\rVert<\varepsilon
.

Complexity:

In
\forall\mathbb{R}
by Koiran
[
Koi99
]
.
\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete by Bürgisser, Cucker
[
BC09
, Proposition 5.2,Corollary 9.3]
, and Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
via reduction from strict positivity over a bounded domain.

Comments:

The tricky part here is membership in
\forall\mathbb{R}
, that follows using Koiran’s method
[
Koi99
]
. Bürgisser, Cucker
[
BC09
]
showed the problem complete for semialgebraic sets defined by arithmetic circuits, Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
strengthened the result to semialgebraic sets defined by formulas.

The directed Hausdorff distance of
S
and
T

(A43) Hausdorff Distance
is
0
if and only if
S\cap T
is dense in
T
, so replacing
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with another semialgebraic set leads to a
\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete problem.

Open Questions:

Does the problem remain hard for algebraic sets?

Also see:

Distance of semialgebraic sets
(A40) Distance of Semialgebraic Sets
, (directed) Hausdorff distance of semialgebraic sets (second level)
(A43) Hausdorff Distance
, zero in closure of semialgebraic set
(A37) Zero in Closure of Semialgebraic Set
, Image density (second level)
(A45) Image Density (arithmetic circuit)
, Domain density (arithmetic circuit)
(A13) Domain Density (arithmetic circuit)
.

(A37) Zero in Closure of Semialgebraic Set

Given:

A semialgebraic set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Question:

Does
0
belong to the closure of
S
? That is, are there
s_{n}\in S
such that
\lVert s_{i}\rVert\rightarrow 0
for
n\rightarrow\infty
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Cucker, Rosselló
[
CR92
]
.

Comments:

Membership is the interesting result here, it requires an upper bound from real algebraic geometry. Cucker and Rosselló
[
CR92
]
call this the
(Euclidean) adherence
problem. Bürgisser, Cucker
[
BC09
, Proposition 5.1,Corollary 9.4]
study both the Euclidean and Zariski adherence problem.

Open Questions:

How hard is this problem in the Zariski topology
[
BC09
, Table 2]
?

Also see:

Distance of semialgebraic sets
(A40) Distance of Semialgebraic Sets
.

(A38) Isolated Point of Semialgebraic Set

Given:

A semialgebraic set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Question:

Is
0
an isolated point in
S
, that is, does
S
not contain other points arbitrarily close to
0
?

Complexity:

\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete by Bürgisser, Cucker
[
BC09
, Corollary 6.7, Corollary 9.4]
via a reduction from testing whether
0
lies in the closure of a semialgebraic set
(A37) Zero in Closure of Semialgebraic Set
.

Comments:

The problem remains hard for algebraic sets, see the isolated zero problem
(A7) Isolated Zero (ISO)
. The complexity of testing whether a semialgebraic set contains an isolated point is open
(A-Open3) Semialgebraic Set with Isolated Point
.

Also see:

Isolated zero
(A7) Isolated Zero (ISO)
, semialgebraic set with isolated point
(A-Open3) Semialgebraic Set with Isolated Point
, zero in closure of semialgebraic set
(A37) Zero in Closure of Semialgebraic Set
.

(A39) Closedness of Basic Semialgebraic Set

Given:

Basic Semialgebraic set
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, a natural number
n\geq 2
.

Question:

Is
B

closed
, that is, does it contain all its limit points?

Complexity:

\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete by Bürgisser, Cucker
[
BC09
, Lemma 6.20,Corollary 9.4]
via a reduction from Feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
.

Comments:

Hardness is easy: let
B=\{(x,y)\in R^{n+1}:f(x)=0,y>0\}
, then
f
is not feasible if and only if the basic semialgebraic set
B
is closed; membership in
\forall\mathbb{R}
is much trickier, and is open for semialgebraic sets in general.
The problem remains
\forall\mathbb{R}
-hard for compactness instead of closedness.

Open Questions:

Does the problem lie in
\forall\mathbb{R}
for semialgebraic sets?

Also see:

Zero in Closure of semialgebraic sets
(A37) Zero in Closure of Semialgebraic Set
.

(A40) Distance of Semialgebraic Sets

Given:

Two semialgebraic sets
S,T\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Question:

Do
S
and
T
have distance
0
, that is, are there
s_{n}\in S
and
t_{n}\in T
such that
\lVert s_{i}-t_{t}\rVert\rightarrow 0
for
n\rightarrow\infty
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ17
]
via a reduction from feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
.

Comments:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-hardness is easy, more interesting is membership in
\exists\mathbb{R}
, since there is a universal quantifier which has to be eliminated. The special case in which
T
consists of a single point amounts to determining whether a point lies in the closure of a semialgebraic set
(A37) Zero in Closure of Semialgebraic Set
.

Also see:

Zero in closure of semialgebraic set
(A37) Zero in Closure of Semialgebraic Set
, Hausdorff distance
(A43) Hausdorff Distance
.

(A41) Hyperplane Local Support of Semialgebraic Set

Given:

A semialgebraic set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, hyperplane
H
.

Question:

Does
H
locally support
S
; that is, some open subset of
H
belongs to
S
and, close to that subset, all of
S
lies on the same side of
H
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Bürgisser, Cucker
[
BC09
]
and Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
via a reduction from feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
.

Comments:

Hardness was shown by Bürgisser and Cucker
[
BC09
]
for arithmetic circuits for a (potentially) larger class. Schaefer and Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
consider semialgebraic sets defined by formulas.

Also see:

Distance of semialgebraic sets
(A40) Distance of Semialgebraic Sets
.

16
Computational Geometry

16.1
Realizability of Configurations

A rank-
d

chirotope
is a function
\chi:\{1,2,\ldots,n\}^{d}:\rightarrow\{-1,0,1\}
with the following properties:
(i) exchanging two components
i
and
j
changes the sign:
\chi(\tau_{ij}(\lambda))=-\chi(\lambda)
, hence it is an
alternating sign map
; (ii) the function
|\chi|
is the characteristic function of independent sets of a
matroid
; (iii) the following
Grassmann-Plücker relation
holds:
for every
\lambda\in[n]^{d-2}
and
a,b,c,d\in[n]\setminus\lambda
, the set

\{\chi(\lambda,a,b)\cdot\chi(\lambda,c,d),-\chi(\lambda,a,c)\cdot\chi(\lambda,b,d),\chi(\lambda,a,d)\cdot\chi(\lambda,b,c)\}

either contains
\{-1,+1\}
or equals
\{0\}
.
We usually identify a chirotope
\chi
with it opposite
-\chi
.
A chirotope defines an oriented matroid of rank
d
. In rank three, chirotopes are often referred to as
order types
.
it can be checked that for a given set of
n
vectors
\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{n}\}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the function
\chi
that returns

\sgn(\det(v_{\lambda_{1}},v_{\lambda_{2}},\ldots,v_{\lambda_{d}}))
satisfies the above properties. This function indicates
the orientation of the simplex formed by any
d
-tuple of vectors. In the plane, we can associate to every point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
the vector
(p_{1},p_{2},1)
. Then the rank-three chirotope given by the determinant indicates whether a given ordered triple of points
forms a clockwise, counter-clockwise, or flat triangle.
It is not true that every rank-three chirotope corresponds to a set of points in the plane, in that sense. Deciding this is one of the fundamental
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete problem.

(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability

Given:

An order type
\chi:[n]^{3}:\rightarrow\{-1,0,1\}
.

Question:

Are there points
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
in the plane realizing this order type, hence such that
p_{k}
lies to the left of
p_{i}p_{j}
if
\chi(p_{i},p_{j},p_{k})=-1
, to the right if
\chi(p_{i},p_{j},p_{k})=1
, and the three points are collinear otherwise?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Mnëv
[
Mnë88
,
Sho91
]
even if the order type is
simple
(aka
uniform
), that is, does not take on value
0
.

Comments:

This is one of the original
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete problems, dual to simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
, and a special case of (rank-
3
) oriented matroid realizability. For a detailed proof of
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness, see Matoušek
[
Mat14
]
. In spatial reasoning, the order type is captured by the
\mathcal{LR}
-calculus (left/right calculus,
(L10)

⁢
L
R

-Calculus
), which is therefore
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
WL10
]
. There is a partial variant, in which
\chi
is only required to be defined on subsets of
[n]
of fixed size and fixed overlap; that version remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
Sch21b
]
, which is useful in establishing results for problems with fixed parameters, such as segment intersection graphs of bounded degree
(CG25) Segment Intersection Graph (SEG)
, pseudo-segment stretchability
(CG10) Pseudo-segment Stretchability
, and the rectilinear local crossing number
(CG51) Rectilinear Local Crossing Number
.

Universality:

Mnëv
[
Mnë88
]
, also see Shor
[
Sho91
]
.

Also see:

Directional walk
(CG6) Directional Walk
, collinearity logic
(L9) Collinearity Logic
,
\mathcal{LR}
-calculus
(L10)

⁢
L
R

-Calculus
, pseudo-segment stretchability
(CG10) Pseudo-segment Stretchability
, rectilinear local crossing number
(CG51) Rectilinear Local Crossing Number
.

(CG2) Oriented Matroid Realizability Over the Reals

Given:

A rank-
d
oriented matroid, for some fixed
d
, given for example as a chirotope
\chi:[n]^{d}\to\{-1,0,1\}
.

Question:

Can this oriented matroid be realized over the reals, hence are there vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\chi(\lambda)=\sgn(\det(v_{\lambda_{1}},v_{\lambda_{2}},\ldots,v_{\lambda_{d}}))
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete from order type realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
.

Also see:

Order type realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
.

(CG3) Matroid Realizability Over the Reals

Given:

A family
F\subset 2^{[n]}
and a dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
.

Question:

Are there vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that for every set
s\in 2^{[n]}
, we have
s\in F
if and only if the vectors
\{v_{i}:i\in S\}
are linearly independent?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Kim, Mesmay, and Miltzow
[
KdMM23
]
; the paper contains two reductions: one from order type realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
, the other from distinct
ETR

(L4) Distinct ETR
.

Comments:

The proof follows from a careful reading of the
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness proofs of order type realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability

[
Mat14
]
, but
the result was first explicitly stated by Kim, Mesmay, and Miltzow
[
KdMM23
]

in an arguably simpler and self-contained way.
Matroids can be represented in various ways, affecting the complexity status of the realizability problem.
Typically matroids are given by a circuit or black-box model. Compared to that,
the explicit representation via independent sets may appear wasteful. However,
representing the problem explicitly an only make it easier, but it is still
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard. Moreover,
matroid realizability is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for every fixed
d\geq 3
, so at most
O(n^{d})
sets have to be specified.

Sturmfels also studied the problem of matroid realizability over the rational numbers and showed similar equivalences with finding rational solutions to Diophantine equations
[
Stu87
]
.
However Sturmfels’ reduction is not polynomial time, so does not establish an
\exists\mathbb{Q}
-completeness result.

Also see:

Order type realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
, distinct
ETR

(L4) Distinct ETR
.

(CG4) (Simple) Allowable Sequence

Given:

A sequence
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k}
of permutations of
[n]
.

Question:

Is the given sequence
allowable
, that is, is there a set of
n
points in the plane so that the permutations
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k}
are obtained, in that order, by projecting the points onto a line rotating by a half turn around some point of the plane?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Hoffmann and Merckx
[
HM18
]
even if the sequence is
simple
, that is, every two consecutive permutations differ by transposition of two adjacent elements via a reduction from order type realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
.

Comments:

Allowable sequences are one of the oldest tools in the computational geometers tool set, first introduced in the 19th century, see
[
GP80
]
.

Universality:

Hoffmann and Merckx
[
HM18
]
.

Also see:

Order type realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
, visibility graph of polygon with holes
(CG36) Visibility Graph of Polygon with Holes
.

(CG5) Radial System Realizability

Given:

An (abstract) radial system, that is a family
(\pi_{i})_{i\in[n]}
such that
\pi_{i}
is a permutation of
[n]-\{i\}
, for each
i
.

Question:

Are there points
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
in the plane so that for each
i
, the points seen from
p_{i}
occur in radial clockwise order as described by
\pi_{i}
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Cardinal and Kusters
[
CK15
]
via a reduction from order type realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
.

Comments:

There is a partial variant, in which the radial system only needs to be specified for point sets of bounded size and overlap, see
[
Sch21b
]
; the partial variant can be used to establish hardness for problems with fixed parameters, such as existence of simultaneous geometric embeddings for a fixed number of graphs, and straight-line realizability of bounded-degree graphs with rotation system
(CG54) Straight-line Realizability of Graph with Rotation System
.

Also see:

Order type realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
, straight-line realizability of graph with rotation system
(CG54) Straight-line Realizability of Graph with Rotation System
, simultaneous geometric embedding
(CG55) Simultaneous Geometric Embedding (SGE)
.

(CG6) Directional Walk

Given:

A walk
W
on
n
vertices, and a direction left/right for every triple of consecutive points along
W
.

Question:

Are there
n
points in the plane so that for all triples
(p,q,r)
of consecutive points along the walk,
r
lies to the left or right or
pq
as specified and distinct vertices are mapped to distinct locations?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
Sch21b
]
via a reduction from a partial version of order type realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
. Remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete even if each edge occurs at most once on the walk
[
KR23
]
.

Comments:

The directional walk problem can be viewed as a sparsified version of order type realizability, or a special case of the left/right calculus
[
WL10
]
.

Open Questions:

Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if the realizing walk has to be crossing-free?

\blacktriangledown
The proof in
[
Sch21b
]
implies that the problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete even if each vertex occurs only a finite number of times along the walk. Is the directional walk problem
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete even if each vertex occurs at most twice (once being trivial)?

Also see:

Order type realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
.

(CG7) Finite Convex Geometry Realizability

Given:

A
finite convex geometry

(V,\mathcal{C})
, that is a set
\mathcal{C}\subseteq 2^{V}
that contains
\emptyset
,
V
, is closed under intersection, and for every
C\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\{V\}
there is an
v\in V
such that
C\cup\{v\}\in\mathcal{C}
.

Question:

Are there
n
points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
which realize the given finite convex geometry? Letting
\conv(C)
denote the convex hull of the points in
C
, a point set
P

realizes
the finite convex geometry
(P,\{C\subseteq P:\conv(C)\cap P=C\})
.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Adaricheva and Wild
[
AW10
,
HM18
]
for
d=2
, via a reduction from order type realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
, and by Hoffmann and Merckx
[
HM18
]
for
d\geq 2
via a reduction from allowable sequence
(CG4) (Simple) Allowable Sequence
.

Also see:

Allowable sequence
(CG4) (Simple) Allowable Sequence
, order type realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
,
convex neural codes
(CG-Open9) Convex Neural Codes
.

(CG8) Free Space Matrix Realizability

Given:

A matrix
M_{\varepsilon}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
,
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
.
M_{\varepsilon}
is the
free space matrix
for two polygonal curves
P=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
and
Q=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{m})
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, if
M_{\varepsilon}
has a
1
in cell
(i,j)
if and only if
\lVert p_{i}-q_{j}\rVert<\varepsilon
.

Question:

Are there two polygonal curves
P
and
Q
so that
M_{\varepsilon}
is the free space matrix for
P
and
Q
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for every
d\geq 2
by Akitaya, Buchin, Mirzanezhad, Ryvkin, and Wenk
[
ABM+23
, Theorem 14]
via a reduction from oriented hyperplane realizability
(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in

R
d

.

Comments:

In
\mathbb{R}^{1}
, the problem can be decided in polynomial time
[
ABM+23
, Theorem 16]
.

Also see:

Free space diagram realizability
(CG-Open5) Free Space Diagram Realizability
, oriented hyperplane realizability
(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in

R
d

.

16.2
Arrangements and Stretchability

Arrangements of vectors or points can be dualized into arrangements of hyperplanes. From the topological representation theorem
[
FL78
,
BKMS05
]
, oriented matroids can always be realized as arrangements of pseudo-hyperplanes. In the planar case, we obtain
pseudoline arrangements
. In a projective setting, a pseudoline arrangement is defined on a two-sphere, but a pseudoline arrangement can also be defined in the plane as a collection of
x
-monotone curves that pairwise intersect exactly once.
For our purpose, the only relevant information about a pseudoline arrangement is the underlying combinatorics, or equivalence class under homeomorphisms of the plane. A convenient way to specify this information is in the form of a
wiring diagram
, or by specifying, for each pseudoline, the left-to-right order of its intersection points with the other pseudolines.

(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability

Given:

A pseudoline arrangement.

Question:

Is the arrangement
stretchable
, that is, is it isomorphic to a straight-line arrangement?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Mnëv
[
Mnë88
]
, and Shor
[
Sho91
]
via a reduction from the existential theory of totally ordered real variables
(L2) Existential Theory of Totally Ordered Real Variables
. The problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if the given arrangement is
simple
, that is, at most two pseudolines intersect in each point
[
SŠ17
]
. Since simple stretchability in the plane is equivalent to simple stretchability in the hyperbolic plane, hyperbolic simple stretchability is also
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
Bie22
,
BBDJ23
]
.

Comments:

Stretchability is the original
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete problem in the Mnëv/Shor tradition. Mnëv
[
Mnë88
]
showed that pseudoline stretchability is universal, and Shor
[
Sho91
]
recast the result from a computer scientists point of view. A nice introductory presentation of the result is due to Matoušek
[
Mat14
]
. Shor
[
Sho91
]
also explicitly shows that pseudo-line stretchability is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

Universality:

Both pseudoline stretchability and simple pseudoline stretchability are universal, as shown by Mnëv
[
Mnë88
]
, also see Shor
[
Sho91
]
.

Also see:

Existential theory of totally ordered real variables
(L2) Existential Theory of Totally Ordered Real Variables
, pseudo-segment stretchability
(CG10) Pseudo-segment Stretchability
.

(CG10) Pseudo-segment Stretchability

Given:

An arrangement of
pseudo-segments
:
x
-monotone curves such that each pair intersects exactly once.

Question:

Is the arrangement
stretchable
, that is, is it isomorphic to an arrangement of straight-line segments?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Mnëv
[
Mnë88
]
, and Shor
[
Sho91
]
, since pseudoline stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
can be viewed as a special case. The problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if each pseudo-segment is involved in at most
72
crossings and the arrangement is simple, by Schaefer
[
Sch21b
]
via a reduction from a partial version of order type realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
. Förster, Kindermann, Miltzow, Parada, Terziadis, and Vogtenhuber
[
FKM+24
]
show that the problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard even if the intersection graph of the arrangement has chromatic number at most
30
.

Comments:

This result can be used to show that segment intersection graphs
(CG25) Segment Intersection Graph (SEG)
of degree at most
216
are
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete to recognize
[
Sch21b
]
and that the geometric thickness of multigraphs
(CG56) Geometric Thickness of Multigraph
is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
FKM+24
]
.

Open Questions:

What is the smallest number of crossings on a pseudo-segment for which the pseudo-segment stretchability remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete?
\blacktriangledown
Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if the intersection graph of the arrangement has chromatic number at most
2
?
[
FKM+24
]
.

Also see:

Order type realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
, geometric thickness of multigraphs
(CG56) Geometric Thickness of Multigraph
, segment intersection graph
(CG25) Segment Intersection Graph (SEG)
.

(CG11) Arrangement Graph

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is
G
an arrangement graph? The vertices of an
arrangement graph
are intersection points of an arrangement
L
of lines in the plane, and two vertices are adjacent if and only if they lie on the same line of
L
and the line segment between them does not intersect any of the lines in
L
.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Bose, Everett, Wismath
[
BEW03
]
via a reduction from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

Also see:

Segment number
(CG43) Segment Number
.

(CG12) (Pseudocircle) Circularizability

Given:

An
arrangement
of
pseudocircles
, that is, a collection of closed curves, any two of which are either disjoint or intersect exactly twice.

Question:

Is the arrangement
circularizable
, that is, is there a homeomorphism of the plane taking it to an arrangement of circles?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Kang and Müller
[
KM14
]
(see also Felsner and Scheucher
[
FS20
]
) via a reduction from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
. The problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard when restricted to arrangements of great pseudocircles
[
FS20
]
.

Also see:

Simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

Given:

A set
S\subseteq\{-,+\}^{n}
with
\{(-,\ldots,-),(+,\ldots,+)\}\subseteq S
.

Question:

Are there
n
oriented hyperplanes in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that for every vector
s\in S
there is a point in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
whose characteristic vector with respect to the hyperplane arrangement is
s
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Tanenbaum, Goodrich, and Scheinerman
[
TGS95
]
, Basri, Felzenszwalb, Girshick, Jacobs, and Klivans
[
BFG+09
]
, and Kang and Müller
[
KM12
]
for every
d\geq 2
via a reduction from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

Comments:

Tanenbaum, Goodrich, and Scheinerman
[
TGS95
]
cast the problem slightly differently, as that of recognizing point-halfspace orders
(CG14) Point-halfspace orders in

R
d

. For
d=2
this is very close to the simple stretchability problem
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
, but with the additional condition that
\{(-,\ldots,-),(+,\ldots,+)\}\subseteq S
. Kang and Müller
[
KM12
]
also show that specifying a realizing hyperplane arrangement may require
2^{\Theta(n)}
bits. Basri, Felzenszwalb, Girshick, Jacobs, and Klivans
[
BFG+09
]
introduce oriented hyperplane realizability under the name matrix visibility problem and show it is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard (they only claim
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness, but reduce from oriented matroid realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
). For
d=2
, McDiarmid and Müller study this as the oriented line arrangement problem.

Also see:

Point-halfspace orders
(CG14) Point-halfspace orders in

R
d

, dot-product graph
(CG24) Dot-Product Graph in

R
d

, unit ball graph
(CG23) Unit Ball Graph in

R
d

, Euclidean preference
(Misc1) Euclidean Preference
, oriented matroid realizability
(CG1) (Simple) Order Type Realizability
.

(CG14) Point-halfspace orders in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

Given:

A bipartite poset
(B,T,\leq)
.

Question:

Are there injective maps
f
from
B
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
g
from
T
to the set of halfplanes in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
f(x)\in g(y)
if and only if
x\leq y
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Tanenbaum, Goodrich, and Scheinerman
[
TGS95
]
via a reduction from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
. Also
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete in any fixed higher dimension
d
.

Comments:

This is a different but essentially equivalent encoding of the oriented hyperplane realizability problem
(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in

R
d

.

Also see:

Oriented hyperplane realizability
(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in

R
d

.

(CG15) Circle Orders

Given:

A poset
P=(S,\leq)
.

Question:

Is
P
the containment order of a set of closed disks in the plane, hence does there exist an injective map
f
from
S
to the set of closed disks in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
f(x)\subseteq f(y)
if and only if
x\leq y
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Cardinal
[
Car24
]
via a reduction from pseudocircle circularizability
(CG12) (Pseudocircle) Circularizability
. Also
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if
P
is a bipartite poset.

Also see:

Point-halfspace orders
(CG14) Point-halfspace orders in

R
d

.

16.3
Covering and Packing

(CG16) Art Gallery Problem

Given:

A simple polygon
P
with corners in
\mathbb{Q}^{2}
and a number
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Question:

Does there exist a set
G\subset P
of
k
guards such that every point inside the polygon is visible from
G
? Two points
a
,
b
are said to be visible to each other in case that the line segment
ab
is completely contained in the polygon
P
. The polygon is considered to be closed.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Abrahamsen, Adamaszek, Miltzow
[
AAM18
,
AAM22
]
, and Stade
[
Sta23
]

Comments:

Also known as the minimum star cover problem
[
O’R87
]
. The original
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness proof in
[
AAM22
]
is fairly involved, but Stade found a shorter proof in 2022
[
Sch23a
]
.
It is known that irrational coordinates can already be necessary for two and three guardable galleries
[
AAM17
,
MM22
]
.
There is a series of papers that try to find practical algorithms for the art gallery problem.
Interestingly some of them come with provable performance guarantees under some mild assumptions
[
HM21
]
.

Universality:

The art gallery problem admits topological universality
[
BEMM+22
,
BEMM+23
,
ST22
]
.

Open Questions:

Does the problem lie in
\exists\mathbb{R}
for semialgebraic art galleries, even in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
?

Also see:

Polygon convex cover
(CG19) Polygon Convex Cover
, star-shapedness of semialgebraic set
(A-Open4) Star-Shapedness of Semialgebraic Set
.

(CG17) Boundary-Guarding

Given:

A simple polygon
P
with corners in
\mathbb{Q}^{2}
and a number
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Question:

Does there exist a set
G\subset\partial P
of
k
guards such that every point inside the polygon is visible by at least one guard in
G
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Stade
[
Sta23
]
via a reduction from
RANGE-ETR-INV

(L7) RANGE-ETR-INV
.

Comments:

The boundary guarding problems differs from the art gallery problem
(CG16) Art Gallery Problem
in that the guards must be on the boundary.

Open Questions:

It is open if boundary-boundary guarding is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete; in the boundary-boundary variant, all guards must be placed on the boundary, and only the boundary has to be guarded
[
Sta23
]
. This is similar to the terrain guarding problem, which turns out to be
\mathsf{NP}
-complete
[
KK11
,
FHKS16
]
.

Also see:

Art gallery problem
(CG16) Art Gallery Problem
,
ETR-INV

(L5) ETR-INV
.

(CG18) Geometric Packing

Given:

A simple polygon
C
, the
container
, and polygonal pieces
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}
, all polygons have corners in
\mathbb{Q}^{2}
.

Question:

Is it possible to move (translate and rotate) all the pieces so they lie in the container without overlapping?

Complexity:

This problem is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Abrahamsen, Miltzow, and Seiferth
[
AMS20
]
, even
if the container is a square and all pieces are convex. The reduction is from
ETR-INV

(L5) ETR-INV
.

Comments:

The problem is in
\mathsf{NP}
in case that only translations are allowed.
The proof from
[
AMS20
]
required that either the pieces or the container had a partially curved boundary. This condition was removed in a paper by Miltzow and Schmiermann
[
MS24
]
about continuous constraint satisfaction problems
(L8) Continuous Constraint Satisfaction Problems (CCSP)
, which implies that geometric packing remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete even if all the pieces are convex and polygonal, a simplified proof of that result can also be found in
[
AMS24
]
.

Open Questions:

Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard if the pieces are restricted to a fixed number of shapes (one, for example)
[
Sch21b
]
?

\blacktriangledown
Is the problem fixed-parameter tractable in the number
m
of pieces?

\blacktriangledown
How hard is the packing problem if the pieces to be packed are unit disks? Hardness may be easier to show for polygons with holes
[
Abr24
]
.

(CG19) Polygon Convex Cover

Given:

A simple polygon, a natural number
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Question:

Can the polygon be written as the union of at most
k
convex polygons?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Abrahamsen
[
Abr22
]
via a reduction from
RANGE-ETR-INV

(L7) RANGE-ETR-INV
. The problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete even if all the convex polygons have to be triangles.

Comments:

Also known as the
minimum convex cover problem
, it was previously known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard
[
CR94
]
and to lie in
\exists\mathbb{R}

[
O’R82
]
. Replacing convex sets with star-shaped sets one obtains the art gallery problem
(CG16) Art Gallery Problem
.

Open Questions:

Abrahamsen
[
Abr24
]
asks whether the polygon cover problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard if a set of convex polygons is given and the original polygon has to be covered by rotated and translated copies of the convex polygons.

Also see:

ETR-INV

(L5) ETR-INV
,
RANGE-ETR-INV

(L7) RANGE-ETR-INV
, art gallery problem
(CG16) Art Gallery Problem
.

16.4
Geometric Representations of Graphs and Hypergraphs

(CG20) Unit Disk Graph

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is
G
the intersection graph of unit disks in the plane?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by McDiarmid, Müller
[
MM10
,
MM13
]
via a reduction from oriented hyperplane realizability
(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in

R
d

. The problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for unit balls in
d
-dimensional space
[
KM12
]
again via a reduction from oriented hyperplane realizability
(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in

R
d

.

Comments:

This problem was earlier shown to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard in
[
BK98
]
.

Open Questions:

Does the problem remain hard for graphs of bounded degree
[
Sch21b
]
?

Also see:

Disk graph
(CG21) Disk Graph
, hyperbolic unit disk graph
(CG22) Hyperbolic Unit Disk Graph
, unit ball graph
(CG23) Unit Ball Graph in

R
d

, unit disk contact graph
(CG-Open3) Unit Disk Contact Graph
, oriented hyperplane realizability
(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in

R
d

.

(CG21) Disk Graph

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is
G
the intersection graph of disks in the plane?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by McDiarmid, Müller
[
MM13
]
via a reduction from oriented hyperplane realizability
(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in

R
d

.

Open Questions:

Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for balls in
d
-dimensional space
[
MM13
]
?
\blacktriangledown
Does the problem remain hard for graphs of bounded degree?

Also see:

Unit disk graph
(CG20) Unit Disk Graph
, unit ball graph
(CG23) Unit Ball Graph in

R
d

.

(CG22) Hyperbolic Unit Disk Graph

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is
G
the intersection graph of unit disks in the hyperbolic plane?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Bieker, Bläsius, Dohse, and Jungeblut
[
BBDJ23
]
via a reduction from hyperbolic simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

Comments:

The proof uses the result that every unit disk graph is also a hyperbolic unit disk graph
[
BFKS23
]
.

Also see:

Unit disk graph
(CG20) Unit Disk Graph
, hyperbolic simple stretchability (under Stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
).

(CG23) Unit Ball Graph in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is
G
the intersection graph of unit balls in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for every fixed
d\geq 2
by Kang, Müller
[
KM12
]
via a reduction from oriented hyperplane realizability
(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in

R
d

. This generalizes the result for unit disk graphs
(CG20) Unit Disk Graph
, the case
d=2
, by McDiarmid and Müller
[
MM10
]
.

Comments:

Kang and Müller
[
KM12
]
call this the
d
-sphericity
problem, and they establish exponential lower and upper bounds on the centers of a family of balls representing
G
. Fiduccia, Scheinerman, Trenk and Zito
[
FSTZ98
]
asked whether the problem lies in
\mathsf{NP}
. For
d=1
we get the
unit interval problem
, which is solvable in linear time
[
LO93
]
.

Open Questions:

Does recognizing unit ball graphs remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete in
d
-dimensional hyperbolic space
[
BBDJ23
]
?

Also see:

Unit disk graph
(CG20) Unit Disk Graph
, oriented hyperplane realizability
(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in

R
d

, dot-product graph
(CG24) Dot-Product Graph in

R
d

.

(CG24) Dot-Product Graph in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

Given:

A graph
G=(V,E)
.

Question:

Are there vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
ij\in E
if and only if
v_{i}^{T}v_{j}\geq 1
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Kang, Müller
[
KM12
]
for every fixed
d\geq 2
via a reduction from oriented hyperplane realizability
(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in

R
d

. This generalizes the result for unit disk graphs
(CG20) Unit Disk Graph
, the case
d=2
, by McDiarmid and Müller
[
MM10
]
.

Comments:

The problem was introduced by Fiduccia, Scheinerman, Trenk and Zito
[
FSTZ98
]
. The smallest
d
for which
G
has a dot-product representation is the
dot-product dimension
of the graph. Kang and Müller
[
KM12
]
also establish exponential upper and lower bounds on the precision of the vectors
v_{i}
.

Also see:

Oriented hyperplane realizability
(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in

R
d

, unit ball graph
(CG23) Unit Ball Graph in

R
d

, unit disk graph
(CG20) Unit Disk Graph
, representing graph with ortogonal vectors
(CG-Open16) Representing Graph with Orthogonal Vectors in

R
3

.

(CG25) Segment Intersection Graph (
SEG
)

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is
G
the intersection graph of line segments in the plane?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Kratochvíl and Matoušek
[
KM94
]
, reduction from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.
The problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard for graphs of bounded degree
[
Sch21b
]
via a reduction from pseudo-segment stretchability
(CG10) Pseudo-segment Stretchability
.

Comments:

A slightly simplified
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hardness proof can be found in
[
Sch10
]
and, in full detail, in
[
Mat14
]
. Kratochvíl and Matoušek
[
KM94
]
show that there are graphs on
n
vertices which require segment representations of size
2^{2^{\Omega(n^{0.5})}}
, and that there are always representations of size at most
2^{2^{O(n)}}
.

Open Questions:

The problem is known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for graphs of girth at least
k

[
KP07
]
, does it remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard?

Also see:

Unit segment intersection graph
(CG26) Unit Segment Intersection Graph
, 3D segment intersection graph
(CG29) 3D Segment Intersection Graph
, planar slope number
(CG40) Planar Slope Number
, pseudo-segment stretchability
(CG10) Pseudo-segment Stretchability
,
k
-polyline intersection graph
(CG27)

k

-Polyline Intersection Graph
, incidence graph of geometric
3
-configuration
(CG-Open15) Incidence Graph of Geometric Configuration
, linear hypergraph realizability
(CG34) Linear Hypergraph Realizability
.

(CG27)
k
-Polyline Intersection Graph

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is
G
the intersection graph of
k
-polylines in the plane? A
k
-polyline
is a polygonal chain consisting of
k-1
line segments.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Hoffmann, Miltzow, Weber, and Wulf
[

for any fixed
k
via a reduction from simple stretchability

(CG28) (Downward) Ray Intersection Graph

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is
G
the intersection graph of (downward) rays in the plane?

Complexity:

The recognition of both ray and downward ray intersection graphs is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Cardinal, Felsner, Miltzow, Tompkins and Vogtenhuber
[
CFM+18
]
via a reduction from pseudoline stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

Comments:

Downward ray intersection graphs are equivalent to grounded segment graphs
[
CFM+18
]
. The paper
[
CFM+18
]
also shows
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness of outer segment graph recognition. Outer segments have one endpoint on a common circle and the other endpoint in the interior of the circle.

Also see:

Segment intersection graph
(CG25) Segment Intersection Graph (SEG)
.

(CG30) 3D Line Intersection Graph

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is

the intersection graph of lines in

?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}

[

(CG31) Convex Set Intersection Graph

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is
G
the intersection graph of a family of convex sets in the plane?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Schaefer
[
Sch10
]
by a reduction from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
. Remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if the graph has bounded degree
[
Sch21b
]
.

Comments:

While not explicitly claimed in
[
Sch10
]
,
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hardness also holds for any family of convex sets that can arbitrarily closely approximate any line segment; this includes ellipses, triangles and convex polygons. It was shown by Müller, van Leeuwen, van Leeuwen
[
MvLvL13
]
that the intersection graph recognition
problem for translates (or homothets) of any fixed convex polygon can be solved on an exponential-size grid.
This implies that the corresponding recognition problems lie in
\mathsf{NP}
.

Open Questions:

Bieker, Bläsius, Dohse, and Jungeblut
[
BBDJ23
]
ask whether the problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete in the hyperbolic plane.

Also see:

Segment intersection graph
(CG25) Segment Intersection Graph (SEG)
, topological inference in RCC8 with convexity
(L13) Topological Inference in RCC8 with Convexity
.

(CG32) Nerves of Convex Set

Given:

A simplicial complex
S
of dimension
k
.

Question:

Is
S
the
k
-skeleton of the nerve of
c
-dimensional convex sets in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for many parameter combinations with
k\geq 2
, in particular

k\geq d
and
c=d-1
or
c=d
, by Schnider and Weber
[
SW23
]
.

Comments:

This defines a family of problems. This family is parameterized by
the dimension of the convex sets
c
,
the ambient dimension
d
containing the convex sets
and the dimension
k
of the dimension of the input simplicial complex.
Note that for
k=1
, this is a question about graphs.
For example,
(k,c,d)=(1,1,1)
asks if the underlying graph is an interval graph,
which is known to be polynomial time solvable
[
BL76
]
.
Another illustrative example is
(k,c,d)=(1,1,2)
. Here, we ask if the underlying graph is the intersection graph
of segments, which is known to be
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
KM94
]
.
The hardness results stem from the hardness results of graphs
combined with a lifting lemma due to Tancer
[
Tan10
]
.
Some other parameter combinations are always possible to realize (specifically
d,c\geq 2k+1

[
Weg67
]
)
and thus the computational problem becomes trivial.

Given a family
A
of sets, we say the nerve of
A
is a simplicial complex that has one vertex for each member of
A
, and any subset
B\subseteq A
forms a simplex if their intersection is non-empty.

Open Questions:

All remaining parameter combinations are still open.

Also see:

Segment intersection graph
(CG25) Segment Intersection Graph (SEG)
, convex set intersection graph
(CG31) Convex Set Intersection Graph
,
convex neural codes
(CG-Open9) Convex Neural Codes
,
finite convex geometry realizability
(CG7) Finite Convex Geometry Realizability
.

(CG33) Hypergraph Induced by Halfspaces and Points

Given:

A hypergraph
H=(V,E)
, an integer
d\geq 0
.

Question:

Is there a set of halfspaces and points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that the induced hypergraph equals
H
? The hypergraph has one vertex per point, and a hyperedge consists of all points belonging to the same halfspace.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Tanenbaum, Goodrich, Scheinerman
[
TGS95
]
via a reduction from pseudoline stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
for every fixed
d\geq 2
. The problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if halfspaces are replaced by balls, ellipsoids and, so-called, bi-curved, difference-separable, and computable convex sets as shown by Bertschinger, El Maalouly, Kleist, Miltzow,
and Weber
[
BEMK+23
]
.

Open Questions:

Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for any other natural geometric objects? The authors of
[
BEMK+23
]
note that they are not aware of any such examples.

Also see:

Stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
, point-halfspace orders
(CG14) Point-halfspace orders in

R
d

, oriented hyperplane realizability
(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in

R
d

.

(CG34) Linear Hypergraph Realizability

Given:

A
linear hypergraph

H
, that is, a hypergraph in which every two edges share at most one point.

Question:

Is there a
straight-line realization
of
H
; that is, can every hyperedge be drawn as a straight-line segment containing all vertices of the hyperedge and no other vertices of
H
? The segments are allowed to cross.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Dobler, Kobourov, Lenhart, Mchedlidze, Nöllenburg, and Symvonis
[
DKL+24
]
via a reduction from matroid realizability over the reals
(CG3) Matroid Realizability Over the Reals

Comments:

Linear hypergraphs of degree at most
2
are always realizable in the sense defined above. The
rank
of a hypergraph is the maximum size of a hyperedge. The straight-line realizability problem becomes non-trivial already for hypergraphs of rank and degree at most
3

[
DKL+24
]
; this corresponds to incidence graphs of geometric
3
-configurations
(CG-Open15) Incidence Graph of Geometric Configuration

Open Questions:

Dobler, Kobourov, Lenhart, Mchedlidze, Nöllenburg, and Symvonis ask whether the problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard if the hypergraph has bounded rank, of even rank
3
. The answer for bounded rank is likely yes, see the comments on a bounded version of collinearity logic
(L9) Collinearity Logic
.

Also see:

Matroid realizability over the reals
(CG3) Matroid Realizability Over the Reals
, collinearity logic
(L9) Collinearity Logic
, segment intersection graph
(CG25) Segment Intersection Graph (SEG)
, incidence graph of geometric
3
-configuration
(CG-Open15) Incidence Graph of Geometric Configuration
.

(CG35) (Point) Visibility Graph

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is
G
the visibility graph of a set of points in the plane? The visibility graph of a set of points in the plane has one vertex per point, and two vertices are adjacent if and only if the line segment between the two points does not contain any other point of the set.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Cardinal and Hoffmann
[
CH17
]
.

Comments:

If the points in the realization have to lie on a grid, the problem becomes
\exists\mathbb{Q}
-complete
[
CH17
]
.

Open Questions:

The complexity of recognizing visibility graphs of polygons is open. In the visibility graph of a polygon, two vertices of the polygon are adjacent if and only if the line segment between them is contained in the polygon.

Also see:

Visibility graph of polygon with holes
(CG36) Visibility Graph of Polygon with Holes
, unit spanning ratio
(CG61) Straight-line Realizability with Unit Spanning Ratio
, segment visibility graph
(CG-Open34) Segment Visibility Graph
, obstacle number
(CG-Open35) Obstacle Number
, hidden set in polygon
(CG-Open23) Hidden Set in Polygon
.

(CG36) Visibility Graph of Polygon with Holes

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is there a polygon with holes such that
G
is the visibility graph of the vertices of
G
; that is, the vertices of
G
are the corners of the polygon and its holes, and there is an edge between two vertices if the segment connecting the two vertices lies inside the polygon?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Hoffmann and Merckx
[
HM18
]
via a reduction from allowable sequence
(CG4) (Simple) Allowable Sequence
and Boomari, Ostovari, and Zarei
[
BOZ18
]
from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

Comments:

For convex polygons with a single hole the visibility graphs are circular arc graphs and therefore recognizable in polynomial time
[
CE95
]
.

Open Questions:

What is the complexity of recognizing visibility graphs of polygons without holes? Boomari, Ostovari, and Zarei
[
BOZ18
]
conjecture that the problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete.

Also see:

Allowable sequence
(CG4) (Simple) Allowable Sequence
, point visibility graph
(CG35) (Point) Visibility Graph
, internal/external polygon visibility graph
(CG37) Internal/External Polygon Visibility Graph
.

(CG37) Internal/External Polygon Visibility Graph

Given:

A graph
G=G_{\mathit{int}}\cup G_{\mathit{ext}}
.

Question:

Is there a simple polygon such that
G_{\mathit{int}}
is the visibility graph of the vertices of
G
inside the polygon, and
G_{\mathit{ext}}
is the visibility graph of the vertices of
G
outside the polygon? Two vertices can see each other if the line segment connecting them does not cut through the side of the polygon.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Boomari, Ostovari, and Zarei
[
BOZ18
]
from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
. The problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete even if the blocking vertex for each pair of vertices which cannot see each other is specified (see
[
BOZ18
]
for definition of blocking vertex).

Open Questions:

What is the complexity of recognizing polygon visibility graphs, that is, if just
G_{\mathit{int}}
is given?

Also see:

Visibility graph of polygon with holes
(CG36) Visibility Graph of Polygon with Holes
, point visibility graph
(CG35) (Point) Visibility Graph
.

(CG38) Triangulated Irregular Network Visibility Graph

Given:

A graph
G
, a plane triangulation
T
.

Question:

Is there a triangulated irregular network (TIN) with visibility graph
G
and
xy
-projection isomorphic to
T
? The
triangulated irregular network (TIN)
assigns coordinates in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
to the vertices of
T
. Two vertices in the TIN can
see
each other if the straight-line segment connecting them lies above the TIN.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Boomari, Ostovari, and Zarei
[

]
from triangulation stretchability

.

Also see:

(CG39) Generalized Transmission Graph

Given:

A directed graph
G=(V,E)
, a family
\mathcal{C}
of geometric objects (e.g. lines in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
).

Question:

Are there objects
C(v)\in\mathcal{C}
and points
p(v)\in C(v)
for every vertex
v\in V
, such that
p(u)\in C(v)
if and only if
uv\in E
for all
u,v\in V
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for
\mathcal{C}
being lines in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by Klost and Mulzer
[
KM18
]
via a reduction from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
. The problem is also
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for
\mathcal{C}
being a restricted type of circular sectors instead of lines
[
KM18
]
.

Open Questions:

For
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness of circular sectors, Klost and Mulzer
[
KM18
]
require a somewhat technical condition (they call wide-spread); they conjecture that that condition can be removed.

\blacktriangledown
What about other natural families of geometric objects? Are there general conditions on
\mathcal{C}
that would guarantee
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hardness?

Also see:

Segment intersection graph
(CG25) Segment Intersection Graph (SEG)
.

16.5
Graph Drawing

Graphs in this section are simple (no multiple edges, no loops) and undirected, unless we say otherwise. A
(topological) drawing
of a graph assigns a different location to each vertex of the graph, typically in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and connects the endpoints of each edge by a (Jordan) curve. We assume that there are no vertices in the interior of edges, edges intersect finitely often, and no more than two edges cross in a point. In a
geometric/straight-line
drawing, all edges are represented by straight-line segments. We refer to crossing-free drawings as
embeddings
or
planar
.

A
rotation
at a vertex is a cyclic permutation of the edges incident on the vertex (meant to represent the order in which the edges leave the vertex in a drawing or embedding). A
rotation system
assigns a rotation to each vertex of a graph.

We will occasionally encounter
\exists\mathbb{Q}
the existential theory of the rationals.

(CG40) Planar Slope Number

Given:

A graph
G
, an integer
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Is there a crossing-free straight-line drawing of
G
in which the edges are drawn using at most
k
different slopes?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Hoffmann
[
Hof17
]
, reduction from pseudoline stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
. For fixed
k
, the problem lies in
\mathsf{NP}

[
Hof17
]
using a result from
[
KM94
]
which shows that intersection graphs of segments with a fixed number of slopes can be recognized in
\mathsf{NP}
.

Comments:

A drawing realizing the planar slope number of the graph may not be possible on a grid, and it is
\exists\mathbb{Q}
-complete to decide whether a grid realization exists, and there is a
2^{2^{\Omega(n^{1/3})}}
lower bound on the grid size
[
Hof17
]
.

Open Questions:

How hard is it to determine the (non-planar) slope number of a graph
[
Hof17
]
?

Also see:

Upward planar slope number
(CG41) Upward Planar Slope Number
, upward outerplanar slope number
(CG-Open14) Upward Outerplanar Slope Number
, segment intersection graph
(CG25) Segment Intersection Graph (SEG)
, line cover number
(CG42) Line Cover Number in

R
d

, segment number
(CG43) Segment Number
, faithful drawability of edge-colored graph
(CG-Open18) Faithful Drawability of Edge-Colored Graph
.

(CG41) Upward Planar Slope Number

Given:

A directed graph
G
, an integer
k\geq 0
. A (straight-line) drawing of
G
is
upward
if
u
lies below
v
for every directed edge
uv\in E(G)
.

Question:

Is there an upward planar straight-line drawing of
G
in which the edges are drawn using at most
k
different slopes?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Quapil
[
Qua24
]
, reduction from pseudoline stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

Comments:

A drawing realizing the planar slope number of the graph may not be possible on a grid, and it is
\exists\mathbb{Q}
-complete to decide whether a grid realization exists, and there is a
2^{2^{\Omega(n^{1/3})}}
lower bound on the grid size
[
Hof17
]
.

Open Questions:

Does the problem turn
\mathsf{NP}
-complete for fixed
k
, as is the case for planar slope number
(CG40) Planar Slope Number
? It is known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard in that case
[
KZ23
]
.
\blacktriangledown
Is the problem
\exists\mathbb{Q}
-complete if the vertices have to lie on a grid?

Also see:

Planar slope number
(CG40) Planar Slope Number
, upward outerplanar slope number
(CG-Open14) Upward Outerplanar Slope Number
.

(CG42) Line Cover Number in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

Given:

A graph
G
, an integer
k\geq 0

Question:

Are there
k
lines in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
whose union contains a crossing-free straight-line drawing of
G
? We say the lines
cover

G
.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Chaplick, Fleszar, Lipp, Ravsky, Verbitsky, and Wolff
[
CFL+23
]
for
d\in\{2,3\}
.

Comments:

Chaplick
et al.

[
CFL+23
]
define the
affine cover number

as the smallest number of
\ell
-dimensional planes in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
that cover a straight-line drawing of
G
. Since the affine cover number of a graph is the same for
d>3
and
d=3
, it is sufficient to study line cover numbers in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and line and plane cover numbers in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Open Questions:

Chaplick
et al.

[
CFL+23
]
show that the plane cover number is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Is it
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete? Is it
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for other values of
(\ell,d)
?

Also see:

Planar slope number
(CG40) Planar Slope Number
, circle cover number under spherical cover number
(CG-Open12) Spherical Cover Number
, minimum line number
(CG-Open13) Minimum Line Number
, planar stick number of drawing
(CG45) Planar Stick Number of Drawing
.

(CG43) Segment Number

Given:

A graph
G
, an integer
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Is there a crossing-free straight-line drawing of
G
which is the union of
k
straight-line segments? The smallest such
k
is known as the
(planar) segment number
.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Okamoto, Ravsky, and Wolff
[
ORW19
]
via a reduction from arrangement graph recognition
(CG11) Arrangement Graph
even if the maximum degree of the graph is
4
and by Erickson
[
Eri19
]
via a reduction from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
if the graph is
4
-regular and given with a fixed embedding which has to be realized. Several variants are
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete as well, including the case that polyline drawings are allowed (
bend segment number
), that crossings are allowed (
crossing segment number
), or that the straight-line drawing lies in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(
3D segment number
)
[
ORW19
]
.

Comments:

Erickson’s result is phrased as straightening a closed curve, see the isotopic polygon problem
(CG44) Isotopic Polygon
. The arc number
(CG-Open11) Arc Number
was introduced by Schulz
[
Sch15
]
as a variation of the segment number.

Also see:

Arrangement graph
(CG11) Arrangement Graph
, arc number
(CG-Open11) Arc Number
, minimum line number
(CG-Open13) Minimum Line Number
, isotopic polygon
(CG44) Isotopic Polygon
, planar stick number of drawing
(CG45) Planar Stick Number of Drawing
.

(CG44) Isotopic Polygon

Given:

A closed curve
\gamma
, that is, a
4
-regular plane graph, an integer
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Is there a polygon with
k
vertices isotopic to
\gamma
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Erickson
[
Eri19
]
via a reduction from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

Comments:

The isotopic polygon problem is a special case of the
planar stick number of drawing
(CG45) Planar Stick Number of Drawing
, and can be viewed as the fixed embedding variant of the segment number
(CG43) Segment Number
, and the

Universality:

Universal
[
Eri19
]
.

Also see:

Segment number
(CG43) Segment Number
, planar stick number of drawing
(CG45) Planar Stick Number of Drawing
.

(CG45) Planar Stick Number of Drawing

Given:

A graph
G
with topological drawing
D
(not necessarily crossing-free), an integer
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Is there a poly-line drawing of
G
isomorphic to
D
with at most
k
segments in total?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete via a reduction from isotopic polygon
(CG44) Isotopic Polygon
.

Comments:

This problem was suggested by the work of Khandhawit, Pongtanapaisan, and Wasun
[
KPW23
]
who introduce the stick number of a spatial diagram of a graph; since this notion requires knot equivalence, there is currently no chance to relate it to
\exists\mathbb{R}
, but the simplified version presented here should still be of interest, as the underlying computational problem. Erickson
[
Eri24
]
observed that hardness follows from the isotopic polygon problem
(CG44) Isotopic Polygon
, even if
G=K_{3}
: start with the closed curve
\gamma
from that problem and view it as a topological drawing of
K_{3}
.

Also see:

Segment number
(CG43) Segment Number
, isotopic polygon
(CG44) Isotopic Polygon
, line cover number
(CG42) Line Cover Number in

R
d

, minimum line number
(CG-Open13) Minimum Line Number
.

(CG46) Triangulation Stretchability

Given:

A plane triangulation
T
, and a collection of paths in
T
.

Question:

Is there a straight-line drawing of
T
in which each path in the collection lies on a line?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Boomari, Ostovari, and Zarei
[
BOZ21
]
from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

Also see:

Triangulated irregular network visibility graph
(CG38) Triangulated Irregular Network Visibility Graph
, simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

(CG47) Graph in a Polygonal Domain

Given:

A graph
G
and a polygon
P
, the
domain
.
Some of the vertices of
G
are specified to be vertices of
P
.

Question:

Does there exists a straight line drawing
D
of
G
such that

D
lies inside the domain
P
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
LMM22
]
via a reduction from
PLANAR-ETR-INV

(L6) PLANAR-ETR-INV
.

Comments:

The problem is closely related to partial drawing extensibility (PDE)
(CG-Open7) Partial Drawing Extensibility
.
In the PDE problem, we are given a partial straight-line drawing of a
graph, and we are asked whether this drawing can be extended to a straight-line drawing of the full graph
[
Pat06
]
.
This differs from the graph in a polygonal domain problem in that in PDE we are
not allowed to place vertices on existing edges, whereas in the current problem we are allowed to place vertices on the already existing polygon.

Open Questions:

Patrignani
[
Pat06
]
showed that the partial drawing extensibility problem
(CG-Open7) Partial Drawing Extensibility
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, is it
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete?

Also see:

Partial drawing extensibility
(CG-Open7) Partial Drawing Extensibility
.

(CG48) Drawing Graph on Segments with Obstacles

Given:

A plane graph
G
, a line-segment
\ell_{v}
(open or closed) for every
v\in V(G)
, and a set
S
of line-segments, all in the plane.

Question:

Is there a straight-line drawing of
G
, isomorphic to the given embedding, in which every vertex
v
lies on
\ell_{v}
, and edges do not cross the line-segments in
S
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Bieker
[
Bie20
]
via a reduction from a variant of
PLANAR-ETR-INV

(L6) PLANAR-ETR-INV
.

Comments:

Since open line segments can be points, this problem can be turned into a restricted version of the partial drawing extensibility problem
(CG-Open7) Partial Drawing Extensibility
, whose complexity is open.

Open Questions:

Segments are allowed to be both open and closed. Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard if all segments are open, or all segments are closed?
\blacktriangledown
The proof requires segments with four different slopes, does the problem become easier with fewer slopes?
\blacktriangledown
Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard if
S
is empty?
\blacktriangledown
Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard if the embedding of the graph is not fixed?

Also see:

Graph in a polygonal domain
(CG47) Graph in a Polygonal Domain
, partial drawing extensibility
(CG-Open7) Partial Drawing Extensibility
, pseudo-segment stretchability
(CG10) Pseudo-segment Stretchability
.

(CG49) Rectilinear Crossing Number

Given:

A graph
G
, an integer
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Does
G
have a straight-line drawing with at most
k
crossings? The smallest such
k
is the
rectilinear crossing number
of
G
.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete.
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hardness was shown by Bienstock
[
Bie91
]
via a reduction from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
and Dean
[
Dea02
]
showed that the problem lies in
\exists\mathbb{R}
.

Comments:

For
k\leq 2
it is known that the rectilinear crossing number equals the standard crossing number
[
BD93
]
, so the problem can be decided in polynomial time for those values. (The case
k=3
is also claimed, but only sketched, in the paper.)

Universality:

Bienstock’s reduction yields algebraic universality
[
Bie91
]
.

Open Questions:

Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for graphs of bounded degree?

\blacktriangledown
Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for fixed
k
? What is the complexity of the problem for
k=4
?

\blacktriangledown
Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if each edge consists of
t
segments, for some fixed
t
. What about
t=2
? This question is based on Bienstock
[
Bie91
]
who named the corresponding crossing number the
t
-polygonal crossing number
, and pointed out that realizing an optimal
t
-polygonal crossing number drawing may require exponential precision for every fixed
t
.

Also see:

Rectilinear local crossing number
(CG51) Rectilinear Local Crossing Number
, pseudolinear vs rectilinear crossing number
(CG50) Pseudolinear vs Rectilinear Crossing Number
, segment intersection graph
(CG25) Segment Intersection Graph (SEG)
, maximum rectilinear crossing number
(CG-Open6) Maximum Rectilinear Crossing Number
.

(CG50) Pseudolinear vs Rectilinear Crossing Number

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

If
G
has a pseudolinear drawing with at most
k
crossings, does
G
have a straight-line drawing with
k
crossings, for every
k
? In other words, does the pseudolinear crossing number of
G
equal its rectilinear crossing number
(CG49) Rectilinear Crossing Number
? A
pseudolinear drawing
of a graph is a pseudoline arrangement in which every edge is drawn as part of a pseudoline.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Hernández-Vélez, Leaños, and Salazar via a reduction from pseudoline stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

Also see:

Rectilinear crossing number
(CG49) Rectilinear Crossing Number
, stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

(CG51) Rectilinear Local Crossing Number

A graph
G
, an integer
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Does
G
have a straight-line drawing with at most
k
crossings
per edge
? The smallest such
k
is the
rectilinear local crossing number
of
G
.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
Sch21a
]
. Remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for fixed
k\geq 867

[
Sch21a
]
.

Comments:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
Sch21a
]
via a reduction from simple pseudo-segment stretchability

.

-complete for
k=1

[
Sch14
]
, and
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for every fixed
k\geq 2

[
Sch21a
]
.

Universality:

Reduction yields algebraic universality
[
Sch21a
]
.

Open Questions:

Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for graphs of bounded degree?
\blacktriangledown
What is the largest
k
for which the problem lies in
\mathsf{NP}
? What is the smallest

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete?

Also see:

, segment intersection graph

(CG52) Geometric Partial
1
-Planarity

Given:

A graph
G=(V,E)
, a subset of edges
F\subseteq E
.

Question:

G
in which every edge of
F
has at most one crossing?

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Schaefer

Sch14
]
via a reduction from pseudoline stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

Comments:

1
-planarity in which
F=E
is only

-complete, a folklore result
[

]
.

, the variant of the problem in which edges in

have to be crossing-free?

(CG53) Weak Rectilinear Realizability

Given:

A graph
G=(V,E)
, a set
R\subseteq\binom{E}{2}
.

Question:

Is there a straight-line drawing of
G
such that
\{e,f\}\in R
for every two edges
e
and
f
that cross in the drawing?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, even for complete graphs by Kynčl
[
Kyn11
]
via a reduction from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
. Remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete in the
strict
case, in which
R
contains exactly the pairs of edges that cross in the drawing, even if
G
is the complete graph.

Comments:

The strict case for a matching is the segment intersection graph problem
(CG25) Segment Intersection Graph (SEG)
, which was known to be
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
KM94
]
; the fact that the problem remains hard for complete graphs is novel here
[
Kyn11
]
. For topological drawings, the problem in
\mathsf{NP}
-complete
[
Kra91b
,
SSŠ03
]
.

Also see:

segment intersection graph
(CG25) Segment Intersection Graph (SEG)
, straight-line realizability of graph with rotation system
(CG54) Straight-line Realizability of Graph with Rotation System
.

(CG54) Straight-line Realizability of Graph with Rotation System

Given:

A graph
G=(V,E)
with
rotation system

\pi
, that is, a function that assigns to every vertex
v\in V
a cyclic permutation of the edges incident on
v
.

Question:

Is there a straight-line drawing of
G
such that the cyclic permutation of the edges incident to each vertex
v
agrees with
\pi(v)
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for complete graphs
[
Kyn11
]
via a reduction from strict rectilinear realizability
(CG53) Weak Rectilinear Realizability
. Remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for graphs of bounded degree
[
Sch21b
]
via a reduction from the partial version of radial system realizability
(CG5) Radial System Realizability

[
Sch21b
]
.

Comments:

For complete graphs, the pairs of intersecting edges completely determine the rotation system of a straight-line drawing and vice versa; this implies that for complete graphs, the problem is equivalent to the strict rectilinear realizability problem.

Open Questions:

How hard is the problem for cubic graphs?
\blacktriangledown
Is it
\mathsf{NP}
-complete to test whether a graph with rotation system has a good (topological) drawing? For complete graphs, this can be decided in polynomial time
[
Kyn20
]
.

Also see:

Weak rectilinear realizability
(CG53) Weak Rectilinear Realizability
, simultaneous geometric embedding
(CG25) Segment Intersection Graph (SEG)
, radial system realizability (partial version)
(CG5) Radial System Realizability
.

(CG55) Simultaneous Geometric Embedding (
SGE
)

Given:

A family
(G_{i})_{i\in[k]}
of graphs on a shared vertex set.

Question:

Is there a straight-line drawing of
G=\bigcup_{i\in[k]}G_{i}
in which each
G_{i}
by itself is planar?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Cardinal and Kusters
[
CK15
]
. Remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete even if
k
is fixed and all graphs are paths
[
Sch21b
]
and in the case that there are no
public
edges (edges belonging to more than one graph), the
empty sunflower case

[
KR23
]
even for fixed
k=31

[
FKM+24
]
.
A weak hardness result for
SGE
was first presented in
[
EBGJ+08
]
.

Comments:

Explicitly shown
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete in
[
CK15
]
via a reduction from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
; the result can also be proved by reduction from strict rectilinear realizability of complete graphs
(CG53) Weak Rectilinear Realizability

[
Kyn11
]
, but the reduction from simple stretchability leads to stronger precision bounds: a simultaneous geometric embedding may require a grid of size
2^{2^{\Omega(n)}}
, where
n=V(G)
. The reductions for fixed
k
start with pseudo-segment stretchability
(CG10) Pseudo-segment Stretchability
.

Open Questions:

Does the empty sunflower case remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard if all graphs are trees?
\blacktriangledown
Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for
k=2

[
Car15
]
?

Also see:

Strict rectilinear realizability
(CG53) Weak Rectilinear Realizability
, geometric thickness of multigraphs
(CG56) Geometric Thickness of Multigraph
, geometric thickness
(CG-Open1) Geometric Thickness
, pseudo-segment stretchability
(CG10) Pseudo-segment Stretchability
.

(CG56) Geometric Thickness of Multigraph

Given:

A multigraph
G
, a natural number
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Question:

Does
G
have a straight-line drawing in which the edges can be colored with
k
colors in such a way that no crossing in the drawing involves two edges of the same color?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Förster, Kindermann, Miltzow, Parada, Terziadis, and Vogtenhuber
[
FKM+24
]
for every fixed
k\geq 57
via a reduction from pseudo-segment stretchability
