(CG10) Pseudo-segment Stretchability
. The problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete even if
G
has fixed local crossing number
[
FKM+24
]
.

Comments:

Geometric thickness was introduced by Kainen
[
Kai73
]
. It has similarities to the simultaneous geometric embedding problem
(CG55) Simultaneous Geometric Embedding (SGE)
, which is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete. For a brief discussion, see
[
Sch21b
, Remark 7]
. The complexity of the geometric thickness of (simple) graphs
(CG-Open1) Geometric Thickness
remains open.

Also see:

Geometric thickness
(CG-Open1) Geometric Thickness
, simultaneous geometric embedding
(CG55) Simultaneous Geometric Embedding (SGE)
.

(CG57) Right-Angle Crossing Drawability

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Does
G
have a straight-line drawing in which all crossings are right-angle crossings? Such drawings are known as right-angle crossing drawings, or
RAC-drawings
, for short.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Schaefer
[
Sch23b
]
via a reduction from feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
(using straight-line drawability with junctions as an intermediate problem). Remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete even if the topological embedding of the graph is given and each edge is involved in at most eleven crossings, and crossing angles are doubly exponentially close to being right-angle
[
Sch23b
]
.

Comments:

Determining whether
G
has a RAC-drawing on the integer grid is
\exists\mathbb{Q}
-complete
[
Sch23b
]
. The
resolution
at a crossing is the smaller of the two angles incident to a crossing; the
crossing resolution
of a straight-line drawing is the largest resolution at any crossing, so RAC-drawings have maximal crossing resolution. The maximal resolution at vertices is known as the angular resolution
(CG59) Angular Resolution
.

Open Questions:

In a RAC
k
drawing each edge is allowed to have up to
k
bends. Are RAC
1
- and RAC
2
-drawability
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard?
\blacktriangledown
Does RAC-drawbility remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard for graphs of bounded degree?
\blacktriangledown
Does RAC-drawability remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard for
1
-planar drawing, that is, drawings with at most one crossing per edge? See geometric partial
1
-planarity
(CG52) Geometric Partial

1

-Planarity
.

\blacktriangledown
Does RAC-drawability remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if all right-angle crossings have to occur between axis-parallel edges? This variant is introduced as apRAC-drawability in
[
ABK+23
]
who showed that the problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

Also see:

Angular resolution
(CG59) Angular Resolution
, geometric partial
1
-planarity
(CG52) Geometric Partial

1

-Planarity
.

(CG58) Lombardi-Drawability With Rotation System

Given:

A graph
G=(V,E)
with
rotation system

\pi
, that is, a function that assigns to every vertex
v\in V
a cyclic permutation of the edges incident on
v
.

Question:

Does
G
have a Lombardi drawing in the plane respecting
\pi
? In a
Lombardi drawing
all edges must be drawn as circular arcs (crossings are allowed), and every vertex must have
perfect angular resolution
, that is, all angles between consecutive ends at the vertex must be the same. The drawing
respects
the rotation system if the cyclic permutation of the edges incident to each vertex
v
agrees with
\pi(v)
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Jungeblut
[
Jun23a
]
via a reduction from simple stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

Comments:

Open Questions:

How hard is Lombardi drawability if no rotation system is given
[
Jun23a
, Open Problem 1]
?
\blacktriangledown
How hard is Lombardi drawability if we require the drawing to be crossing-free (with or without rotation system)n
[
Jun23a
, Open Problem 2]
?

\blacktriangledown
Does Lombardi drawability remain hard for graphs of bounded degree?

Also see:

Straight-line realizability of graph with rotation system
(CG54) Straight-line Realizability of Graph with Rotation System
, angular resolution
(CG59) Angular Resolution
, arc number
(CG-Open11) Arc Number
.

(CG59) Angular Resolution

Given:

A graph
G
, a natural number
k\geq 2
.

Question:

Does
G
have a straight-line drawing with
angular resolution
at least
\pi/(2k)
, that is, a drawing in which all angles between incident edges are at least
\pi/(2k)
? The
angular resolution
of
G
is the supremum of the angular resolution of all straight-line drawings of
G
.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Schaefer
[
Sch23a
]
via a reduction from feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
(using straight-line drawability with junctions as an intermediate problem). Remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete even if a crossing-free topological embedding of the graph is given
[
Sch23a
]
.

Comments:

There is a subtle difference between a graph having angular resolution at least
\alpha
and there being a drawing of the graph with angular resolution at least
\alpha
, since angular resolution of a graph is defined as a supremum, as observed in
[
FHH+93
]
. Both versions are
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete.

Open Questions:

Testing for angular resolution at least
\pi/4
is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete. Is the angular resolution problem
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for
\pi/3

[
Sch23a
]
?
\blacktriangledown
Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if
G
is planar, but the angular-resolution drawing does not have to be
[
Sch23a
]
?

Also see:

RAC-drawability
(CG57) Right-Angle Crossing Drawability
.

(CG60) Prescribed Area Extension

Given:

A plane graph
G
, an area assignment for
G
, where an
area assignment
for
G
assigns a positive real number to every face of
G
; locations for some of the vertices of
G
are fixed in the plane.

Question:

Does
G
have a straight-line drawing realizing the area assignment, that is, each face having the area prescribed by the area assignment, with the fixed vertices at their prescribed location?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Dobbins, Kleist, Miltzow and Rzążewski
[
DKMR23
]
via a reduction from
PLANAR-ETR-INV

(L6) PLANAR-ETR-INV
.

Open Questions:

Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard without fixing vertex locations?

Also see:

Prescribed area
(CG-Open32) Prescribed Area
, area universality
(CG-Open33) Area Universality
, area universality for triples with partial area assignment
(CG75) Area Universality For Triples with Partial Area Assignment
.

(CG61) Straight-line Realizability with Unit Spanning Ratio

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is there a
proper
straight-line drawing of
G
in the plane with spanning ratio one? Here proper means that no two vertices are drawn on the same point, and no point corresponding to a vertex lies on a segment representing an edge, apart from the two endpoints. The
spanning ratio
of a drawing is the maximum ratio, over all pairs of vertices, of the length of a shortest path in the drawing and the Euclidean distance.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Aichholzer, Borrazzo, Bose, Cardinal, Frati, Morin, Vogtenhuber
[
ABB+22
]
via a reduction from the point visibility graph problem
(CG35) (Point) Visibility Graph
.

Comments:

Aichholzer et al.
[
ABB+22
]
show that a graph has a proper drawing with spanning ratio
1
if and only if it is the visibility graph of a set of points in the plane
(CG35) (Point) Visibility Graph
. They also show that the problem becomes linear-time solvable if the drawing has to be planar (crossing-free).

Also see:

Point visibility graph
(CG35) (Point) Visibility Graph
.

(CG62) Graph Realizability

Given:

A graph
G
with weight function
\ell:E(G)\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.

Question:

Is there a straight-line drawing of
G
in which every edge
e
of
G
has length
\ell(e)
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Schaefer
[
Sch13
]
via a reduction from pseudoline stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
. The problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete even if
\ell(e)=1
for all
e\in E(G)
.

Comments:

Graph drawings are assumed to be non-degenerate in that vertices do not overlap with other vertices or edges; relaxing that condition leads to the linkage realizability problem
(CG65) Linkage Realizability
.
The problem was first shown
\mathsf{NP}
-hard by Saxe
[
Sax79
]
and Yemini
[
Yem79
]
; Eades and Wormald
[
EW90
]
showed
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness if the drawing has to be crossing-free (and all edges have the same length), and that result was strengthened by Cabello, Demaine, and Rote
[
CDR07
]
. The problem is known under different names to different communities, e.g. as the Euclidean distance matrix completion problem
[
Lau09
]
, and it has many variants and applications
[
LLMM14
]
. A related problem is the reconstruction problem: given a multiset of distances (of the form
\sqrt{q}
for
q\in\mathbb{Q}
) are there
n
points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that the multiset contains exactly their pairwise distances. This problem turns out to be
\mathsf{NP}
-complete
[
LSS03
]
.

Open Questions:

It is open whether the problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for embedded graphs.
(A claimed
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hardness proof
[
ADD+16
]
may not be complete. We were informed by one of the authors
that the PhD thesis of Abel
[
Abe16
]
contains more details, “ but is still lacking some detail and edits”.)

Also see:

Unit distance graph
(CG64) (Strict) Unit Distance Graph
, linkage realizability
(CG65) Linkage Realizability
, graph flattenability
(CG-Open17) Graph Flattenability
.

(CG63) Graph with Long and Short Edges

Given:

A graph
G=(V,E)
with a partition of the edges
E=E_{s}\cup E_{\ell}
into
short
and
long
edges.

Question:

Is there a straight-line drawing of
G
in which exactly the short edges have length at most
1
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Angelini, Cornelsen, Haase, Katsanou, Montecchiani, Symvonis
[
ACH+24
]
via a unit disk graph
(CG20) Unit Disk Graph
.

Comments:

Graphs with long and short edges were introduced in
[
ACH+24
]
under the name
dichotomous ordinal graphs
. The same paper suggests a reduction from Euclidean preference
(Misc1) Euclidean Preference
, but there also is a direct reduction from unit disk graphs
(CG20) Unit Disk Graph
:
G=(V,E)
is a unit disk graph if and only if the graph
H
on vertex set
V
can be realized with short edges
E
and long edges
\binom{V}{2}-E
.

Open Questions:

Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if the realization has to be crossing-free? If the short edges have to induce a plane graph, then, as
[
ACH+24
]
point out, the problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard
[
AKPT16
]
.

\blacktriangledown
Does the problem remain
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if the underlying graph is a complete bipartite graph? That is the case of most interest in
[
ACH+24
]
.

Also see:

Unit disk graph
(CG20) Unit Disk Graph
, Euclidean preference
(Misc1) Euclidean Preference
, graph realizability
(CG62) Graph Realizability
.

(CG64) (Strict) Unit Distance Graph

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is
G
a (strict) subgraph of
E_{2}=(\mathbb{R}^{2},\{uv\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{2}:|u-v|=1\})
?

Complexity:

Recognition of both unit distance graphs and strict unit distance graphs is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Schaefer
[
Sch13
]
via a reduction from graph realizability
(CG62) Graph Realizability
.

Comments:

By definition, different vertices of
G
correspond to distinct points in
E_{2}
; if we relax this condition, we get the degenerate unit distance graph problem
[
HKP11
]
, which we discuss as part of the linkage realizability problem
(CG65) Linkage Realizability
. Erdös, Haray and Tutte
[
EHT65
]
introduced the
Euclidean dimension
of a graph, which is the smallest
d
for which
G
is a subgraph of
E_{d}=(\mathbb{R}^{d},\{uv\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}:|u-v|=1\})
; so testing whether a graph has dimension at most
2
is already
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete.

Universality:

Maehara
[
Mae91
]
showed that the distances that occur in rigid unit distance graphs are exactly the algebraic numbers.

Open Questions:

Is the Euclidean dimension
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for every fixed
d
?

Also see:

Linkage realizability
(CG65) Linkage Realizability
, unit disk contact graph
(CG-Open3) Unit Disk Contact Graph
, graph realizability
(CG62) Graph Realizability
.

(CG65) Linkage Realizability

Given:

A graph
G
with a function
\ell:E(G)\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.

Question:

Can we assign a point
p_{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to every
u\in V(G)
such that
|f(u)-f(v)|=\ell(uv)
for every edge
uv
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Abbott
[
Abb08
]
via a reduction from Feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
for every
d\geq 2
, The problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete even for unit linkages , that is if
\ell(e)=1
for all
e\in E(G)
, in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by Schaefer
[
Sch13
]
via a reduction from pseudoline stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

Comments:

The main difference to graph realizability
(CG62) Graph Realizability
is that vertices can overlap with other vertices and edges. In some definitions, linkages also come with points with pre-assigned locations. Unit linkages are called
degenerate unit distance graphs
by Horvat, Kratochvíl, and Pisanski
[
HKP11
]
who showed that the problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

Universality:

The universality of linkage realizability is Kempe’s universality theorem
[
KM02
,
Abb08
]
.

Open Questions:

Is realizability of unit linkages
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete in arbitrary dimension? There is a gadget, due to Saxe
[
Sax79
]
, that increases the dimension by one, but it is not a unit linkage.

Also see:

Unit distance graph
(CG64) (Strict) Unit Distance Graph
, graph realizability
(CG62) Graph Realizability
, linkage rigidity
(CG66) Linkage Rigidity
.

(CG66) Linkage Rigidity

Given:

A graph
G
with a function
\ell:E(G)\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
, and a
configuration
, that is,
p_{u}\in\mathbb{Q}^{d}
for every
u\in V(G)
such that
|p_{u}-p_{v}|=\ell(uv)
for every edge
uv
.

Question:

Is the configuration rigid, that is, do small changes to all
p_{u}
so that the length conditions are still satisfied lead to the same configuration modulo rigid motions of the plane?

Complexity:

\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete by Abbott
[
Abb08
]
, Schaefer
[
Sch13
]
for
d\geq 2
via a reduction from
ISO

(A7) Isolated Zero (ISO)
. Abbott
[
Abb08
]
shows that
ISO
reduces to linkage rigidity for
d=2
, and
d-1
-dimensional linkage rigidity reduces to
d
-dimensional linkage rigidity. Together with the
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness of
ISO
, this implies
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness of
d
-dimensional linkage rigidity for every fixed
d\geq 2
. Membership of linkage rigidity in
\forall\mathbb{R}
is shown in
[
Sch13
]
.

Comments:

The main difference to graph realizability
(CG62) Graph Realizability
is that vertices can overlap with other vertices and edges.

Also see:

Unit distance graph
(CG64) (Strict) Unit Distance Graph
, linkage realizability
(CG65) Linkage Realizability
, graph realizability
(CG62) Graph Realizability
.

16.6
Polytopes and Simplicial Complexes

A polytope is the convex hull of a finite point set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The
face lattice
of a polytope is the lattice of its faces: the
join

of two faces is the smallest face containing both faces, and the
meet

is the intersection of the faces. Given a lattice, that is, a partial order with
join and meet operations, one can ask whether it is
polytopal
, namely
whether it is the face lattice of a polytope.
We refer to the face lattice of
P
as its
combinatorial type
, and to
P
itself as a

realization
of this combinatorial type.
The realizability question is known as the
algorithmic Steinitz problem
, due to a famous theorem of Steinitz.
Let the
graph of a polytope
be the graph formed by the vertices and edges of the
polytope. The graph is also referred to as the
1
-skeleton
, and the
k
-skeleton for
k\geq 1
is the
complex of all faces of dimension up to
k
.
Steinitz theorem characterizes the graphs of
3
-dimensional polytopes:
they are precisely the simple, planar,
3
-connected graphs
[
Zie95
, Section 4]
, hence easy to
recognize. For
3
-polytopes the facial lattice is determined by the graph of the polytope,
so the algorithmic Steinitz problem for
3
-polytopes is tractable.

Note that the complete combinatorial type of a polytope can be deduced from its set of
(n-1)
-dimensional faces, or
facets
: the lower-dimensional faces are simply obtained by intersecting subsets of facets. The input to the problems can therefore be restricted to be a list of facets.

We obtain an interesting variant if we restrict the problem to
simple
polytopes, the skeletons of which are regular graphs. In that case the facial lattice is determined by the graph of the polytope
(Blind and Mani with an easy proof by Kalai, see
[
Zie95
, Section 3.4]
and
[
KP03
, Problem 15]
).

A polytope is said to be
simplicial
if all its facets are simplices.

Subdivisions
are closely related to polytopes. Given a set of points, a
subdivision is a covering of their convex hull by polytopes, or
cells
, the vertices of which are
points of the set, such that any two intersect in a common face.
If all cells are simplices, then the subdivision is called a
triangulation
.
A
Delaunay subdivision
is such that the cells are defined by an empty sphere condition:
A cell
C
is part of the Delaunay subdivision of a set of points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
if and only if there exists a

(d-1)
-sphere passing through the vertices of
C
but containing no other point of the set in its interior.
For generic point sets, such cells are always simplices, and we talk of
Delaunay triangulations
.

An
abstract simplicial
k
-complex
is a collection of sets of size at most
k
that is closed under taking subsets. More generally, an
(abstract) polyhedral complex
is a collection of (combinatorial types of) polytopes, such that every face of every polytope is also part of the collection, and any two polytopes in the collection intersect in a common face.

(CG67) Polytope Realizability

Given:

A combinatorial type, given as a list of facets.

Question:

Does there exist a polytope of this combinatorial type?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Mnëv
[
Mnë88
]
via a reduction from existential theory of totally ordered real variables
(L2) Existential Theory of Totally Ordered Real Variables
.

Comments:

This problem is also known as the
algorithmic Steinitz problem
. The problem is known to be hard in particular for
n
-dimensional polytopes with

n+4
vertices. The input is then a collection of subsets of size at least
n
of the
n+4
vertices.

Universality:

Mnëv
[
Mnë88
]
, also see Richter-Gebert
[
RG99
]
.

Also see:

4-dimensional Polytope Realizability
(CG68) 4-dimensional Polytope Realizability
, Simplicial Polytope Realizability
(CG69) Simplicial Polytope Realizability
, Inscribed Simplicial Polytope Realizability
(CG70) Inscribed Simplicial Polytope Realizability
, treetope graph
(CG-Open37) Treetope graph
, existential theory of totally ordered real variables
(L2) Existential Theory of Totally Ordered Real Variables
.

(CG68) 4-dimensional Polytope Realizability

Given:

A combinatorial type, given as a list of facets.

Question:

Does there exist a 4-dimensional polytope of this combinatorial type?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Richer-Gebert and Ziegler
[
RGZ95
]
and Richter-Gebert
[
RG96
]
via a reduction from existential theory of totally ordered real variables
(L2) Existential Theory of Totally Ordered Real Variables
.

Universality:

Richter-Gebert and Ziegler
[
RGZ95
]
, and Richter-Gebert
[
RG96
,
RG99
]
.

Open Questions:

The complexity of the problem for simplicial polytopes of constant dimension
d\geq 4
is open, see Richter-Gebert
[
RG96
]
. The complexity of the related problem in dimension 4 in which we are only given the graph (
1
-skeleton) of the polytope is also open.

Also see:

Polytope Realizability
(CG67) Polytope Realizability
.

(CG69) Simplicial Polytope Realizability

Given:

A combinatorial type, given as a list of facets.

Question:

Does there exist simplicial polytope of this combinatorial type?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Mnëv
[
Mnë88
]
, and Adiprasito and Padrol
[
AP17
]
even in even dimension via a reduction from oriented matroid realizability
(CG2) Oriented Matroid Realizability Over the Reals
.

Universality:

Adiprasito and Padrol
[
AP17
]
.

Comments:

Universality of simplicial polytopes was announced by Mnëv
[
Mnë88
]
, but with an incomplete proof. Adiprasito and Padrol prove the universality result for
neighborly polytopes
, the
d
-dimensional polytopes whose
\lfloor d/2\rfloor
-skeleton is complete. Neighborly polytopes in even dimension are simplicial, so this implies the current result, but we can further assume neighborliness.
The problem is known to be hard in particular for
n
-dimensional polytopes with

n+4
vertices.

Open Questions:

Encouraged by the results of Richter-Gebert
[
RG96
]
and Bokowski and Guedes de Oliveira
[
BGdO90
]
, Adiprasito and Padrol make the “daring conjecture” that the result holds in dimension
4
.

Also see:

Inscribed Simplicial Polytope Realizability
(CG70) Inscribed Simplicial Polytope Realizability
.

(CG70) Inscribed Simplicial Polytope Realizability

Given:

A combinatorial type, given as a list of facets.

Question:

Does there exist simplicial polytope of this combinatorial type, all vertices of which lie on a sphere?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Adiprasito, Padrol, and Theran
[
APT15
]
.

Universality:

Adiprasito, Padrol, and Theran
[
APT15
]
.

Comments:

Delaunay subdivisions are obtained by stereographic projections of inscribed polytopes.
The problem is known to be hard in particular for
n
-dimensional polytopes with

n+4
vertices.

Open Questions:

The complexity of the problem for inscribed polytopes (not necessarily simplicial) of constant dimension
d\geq 4
is open as well. In dimension
3
, a result by Rivin implies that inscribed polytopes can be recognized in polynomial time
[
Riv96
]
, although it is not clear how to efficiently produce a realization.

Also see:

Delaunay Triangulation
(CG72) Delaunay Triangulation
.

(CG71) Nested Polytopes

Given:

Two polytopes
A\subseteq B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a number
k\in\mathbb{N}
. The polytope
A
is described by vertices and the polytope
B
is described by its facets.

Question:

Does there exist a polytope
X
on at most
k
vertices with
A\subseteq X\subseteq B
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Dobbins, Holmsen and Miltzow
[
DHM19
]
via a direct reduction from
ETR-INV

(L5) ETR-INV
.

Universality:

Universality follows from
[
DHM19
]
.

Comments:

Vavasis
[
Vav09
]

showed that there is a reduction from non-negative matrix factorization
(A21) Non-negative Matrix Factorization (NMF)
to nested polytope,
for the special case
k=d+1
.
(In the case
k=d-1
the intermediate polytope is a simplex, and thus they call this problem the intermediate simplex problem.
)

Shitov showed in turn
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness and universality for non-negative matrix factorization and thus also implied the nested polytope result
[
Shi16b
]
.

Also see:

ETR-INV

(L5) ETR-INV
.

(CG72) Delaunay Triangulation

Given:

A collection
F
of subsets of size
n-4
of
[n]
.

Question:

Does there exist a
(n-5)
-dimensional Delaunay triangulation on
n
vertices, such that the vertices of the cells of the triangulations are exactly the subsets in
F
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Adiprasito, Padrol, and Theran
[
APT15
]
via a reduction from Inscribed Simplicial Polytope Realizability
(CG70) Inscribed Simplicial Polytope Realizability
.

Universality:

Adiprasito, Padrol, and Theran
[
APT15
]
.

Comments:

The result is directly reduced from the complexity of Inscribed Simplicial Polytope Realizability
(CG70) Inscribed Simplicial Polytope Realizability
: An inscribed polytope in dimension
d
is mapped to a Delaunay subdivision in dimension
d-1
via a stereographic projection.

Open Questions:

The complexity of the problem in constant dimension
d\geq 3
is open. In dimension two, it is known from a result by Rivin that Delaunay triangulations can be recognized in polynomial time
[
Riv96
]
, although it is not clear how to efficiently produce a realization. Adiprasito, Padrol, and Theran conjecture that universality already holds for
3
-dimensional Delaunay subdivisions
[
APT15
]
. Padrol and Theran
[
PT14
]
give examples of Delaunay triangulations with disconnected realization spaces.

Also see:

Inscribed Simplicial Polytope Realizability
(CG70) Inscribed Simplicial Polytope Realizability
, Gabriel graph
(CG-Open22) Gabriel Graph
.

(CG73) Simplicial Complex

Given:

An abstract simplicial
k
-complex
C
and a natural number
d\in\mathbb{N}
.

Question:

Does
C
have a geometric embedding in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for all
d\geq 3
and
k\in\{d-1,d\}
, by Abrahamsen, Kleist, and Miltzow
[
AKM23
]
via a reduction from stretchability
(CG9) (Pseudoline) Stretchability
.

Comments:

For piecewise linear embedding, the problem has been shown to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard even in dimension 3, see
[
MTW11
,
dMRST20
]
.

Open Questions:

For which combinations of
k
and
d
is the problem
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete?
An interesting case is when
k=2
and
d=4
.

Also see:

Polyhedral complex
(CG74) Polyhedral Complex
.

(CG74) Polyhedral Complex

Given:

An abstract polyhedral complex
C
composed of quadrangles and triangles.

Question:

Does
C
have a geometric embedding in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
in which the image of every polygon is a convex polygon, and their relative interiors are disjoint?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, by Brehm (unpublished), via a reduction from collinearity
(L9) Collinearity Logic
.

Comments:

The result has been announced and presented, but never published formally. It is hinted at by Ziegler
[
Zie08
]
. Wilson
[
Wil12
]
also showed that the same result holds for polyhedral complexes composed of convex three-dimensional polyhedra.

Also see:

Collinearity logic
(L9) Collinearity Logic
, simplicial complex
(CG73) Simplicial Complex
.

17
Game Theory

17.1
Games in Strategic Form

We briefly recall the definitions related to games given in
strategic form
.
An
r
-player game
\Gamma
in strategic form is a pair
\Gamma=(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{U}_{i})
, for
i\in 1,2,\ldots,r
, where
\Sigma_{i}
is the set of strategies available to the
i
th player, and
\mathsf{U}_{i}
is the payoff function of player
i
, of the form
\mathsf{U}_{i}:\Sigma_{1}\times\Sigma_{2}\times\ldots\times\Sigma_{r}\to\mathbb{R}
.

A
mixed strategy
for the
i
th player, as opposed to a
pure strategy

s\in\Sigma_{i}
, is a probability distribution
\sigma_{i}
on the pure strategies in
\Sigma_{i}
. Let
\mathbf{\sigma}=(\sigma_{i})_{i=1,2,\ldots,r}
be a collection of mixed strategies for each of the
r
players. The expected payoff of the
i
th player under these probability distributions is:

\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma})=\sum_{s\in\prod_{i=1}^{r}\Sigma_{i}}\mathsf{U}_{i}(s)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{r}\sigma_{i}(s_{i})\cdot

A Nash equilibrium is a collection of mixed strategies such that no player can improve her expected payoff by deviating from her current mixed strategy. Hence it consists of a collection of mixed strategies
\mathbf{\sigma}
such that for every
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\}
and every
\mathbf{\sigma}^{\prime}
obtained by replacing
\sigma_{i}
by another mixed strategy
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
, we have
\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma})\geq\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma}^{\prime})
. The classical result of Nash is that such an equilibrium exists for any game.
A Nash equilibrium is said to be
pure
if it consists only of pure strategies.

(GT1) Nash Equilibrium in a Ball

Given:

A
3
-player game
\Gamma
in strategic form,
u\in\mathbb{Q}
.

Question:

Does
\Gamma
have a Nash equilibrium
\mathbf{\sigma}
such that
\sigma_{i}(s)\leq u
for all
i=1,2,3
and all
s\in\Sigma_{i}
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ17
]
via a reduction from Brouwer Fixed Point
(A5) Brouwer Fixed Point
due to Ettessami and Yannakakis
[
EY10
]
.

Comments:

The result holds for a fixed
u=1/2

[
SŠ17
]
.

Also see:

Brouwer Fixed Point
(A5) Brouwer Fixed Point
.

(GT2) Nash Equilibrium with Bounded Payoffs

Given:

A
3
-player game
\Gamma
in strategic form,
u\in\mathbb{Q}
.

Question:

Does
\Gamma
have a Nash equilibrium
\mathbf{\sigma}
such that
\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma})\geq u
for all
i\in\{1,2,3\}
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Garg, Mehta, Vazirani, Yazdanbod
[
GMVY18
]
via a reduction from Nash Equilibrium in a ball
(GT1) Nash Equilibrium in a Ball
.

Comments:

The problem in which we require that
\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma})\leq u
instead is also
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete. Both results hold for symmetric win-lose games as well, as shown by Bilò, Hansen, Arnsfelt and Mavronicolas
[
BHM23
]
. Berthelsen and Hansen
[
BH22
]
also show that for zero-sum games, deciding the existence of a Nash equilibrium in which
\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma})=0
for all
i\in\{1,2,3\}
is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete. The proofs of the latter are related to those in Hansen
[
Han19
]
.

(GT3) Nash Equilibrium with Restricted Support

Given:

A
3
-player game
\Gamma
in strategic form, a subset
T_{i}\subseteq\Sigma_{i}
for each
i=1,2,3
.

Question:

Does
\Gamma
have a Nash equilibrium
\mathbf{\sigma}
such that the set of strategies
s\in\Sigma_{i}
such that
\sigma_{i}(s)>0
is contained in
T_{i}
for every
i\in\{1,2,3\}
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Garg, Mehta, Vazirani, Yazdanbod
[
GMVY18
]
via a reduction from Nash Equilibrium in a ball
(GT1) Nash Equilibrium in a Ball
.

Comments:

The problem in which we require that the set contains
T_{i}
instead is also
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
GMVY18
,
BM21
]
. The two problems are respectively called
Superset
and
Subset
in
[
GMVY18
]
.
Both results hold for win-lose games as well
[
BHM23
]
.

(GT4) Second Nash Equilibrium

Given:

A
3
-player game
\Gamma
in strategic form.

Question:

Does
\Gamma
have at least two distinct Nash equilibria?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Garg, Mehta, Vazirani, Yazdanbod
[
GMVY18
]
via a reduction from Nash Equilibrium with Bounded Payoffs
(GT2) Nash Equilibrium with Bounded Payoffs
. The problem is referred to as
NonUnique
in
[
GMVY18
]
.

(GT5) Nash Equilibrium with Bounded Total Payoff

Given:

A
3
-player game
\Gamma
in strategic form,
u\in\mathbb{Q}
.

Question:

Does
\Gamma
have a Nash equilibrium
\mathbf{\sigma}
such that
\sum_{i\in\{1,2,3\}}\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma})\geq u
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Bilò, Mavronicolas
[
BM21
]
via a reduction from Nash Equilibrium with Restricted Support
(GT3) Nash Equilibrium with Restricted Support
.

Comments:

The problem in which we require that
\sum_{i}\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma})\leq u
instead is also
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete.
An alternative hardness proof is also given by Berthelsen and Hansen
[
BH22
]
.
Both results hold for symmetric win-lose games as well
[
BHM23
]
.

(GT6) Nash Equilibrium with Bounded Support

Given:

A
3
-player game
\Gamma
in strategic form, a natural number
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Question:

Does
\Gamma
have a Nash equilibrium
\mathbf{\sigma}
such that the number of nonzero components of
\sigma_{i}
is at least
k
, for every
i\in\{1,2,3\}
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Bilò, Mavronicolas
[
BM21
]
via a reduction from Nash Equilibrium with Restricted Support
(GT3) Nash Equilibrium with Restricted Support
.

Comments:

The problem in which we require that the support has size at most
k
is also
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete. Both results hold for symmetric win-lose games as well
[
BHM23
]
.

(GT7) Minmax Value

Given:

A 3-player game
\Gamma
in strategic form.

Question:

Is the minmax value of player 1 at most 0? Here the minmax value of player
i
is the minimum utility
\min_{\sigma}\max_{s_{i}}\mathsf{U}_{i}(\sigma\setminus s_{i})
of a best reply strategy, where
\sigma\setminus s_{i}
is the collection of strategies obtained by replacing
\sigma_{i}
by
s_{i}
in
\sigma
.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Hansen
[
Han19
]
via a reduction from a variant of
(A1) Feasibility
similar to that of
[
Sch13
, Lemma 3.9]
, where the solution of a system of quadratic equations is promised to lie in a polyhedron contained in the standard simplex.

Comments:

The problem remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete under various promises, see Hansen
[
Han19
]
for details.

(GT8) Trembling Hand Perfect Equilibrium

Given:

A 3-player game
\Gamma
in strategic form, and a pure Nash equilibrium
\mathbf{\sigma}
of
\Gamma
.

Question:

Is
\mathbf{\sigma}
a trembling hand perfect equilibrium?
A collection of strategies
\sigma
is a trembling hand perfect equilibrium if and only if it is the limit of a sequence of
\varepsilon
-perfect equilibria with
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, where an
\varepsilon
-perfect equilibrium is a Nash equilibrium that is fully mixed, and in which only pure strategies that are best replies get probability more than
\varepsilon
, see
[
Sel75
,
Mye78
]
.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Hansen
[
Han19
]
via a reduction from Minmax value
(GT7) Minmax Value
.

(GT9) Proper Equilibrium

Given:

A 3-player game
\Gamma
in strategic form, and a pure Nash equilibrium
\mathbf{\sigma}
of
\Gamma
.

Question:

Is
\mathbf{\sigma}
a proper equilibrium? Here
\sigma
is a proper equilibrium if it is the limit point of a sequence of
\varepsilon
-proper equilibria with
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, where an
\varepsilon
-proper equilibrium is a fully mixed equilibrium such that given two pure strategies
k
and
\ell
of the same player, if
k
is a worse reply against
\sigma
than
\ell
, then
\sigma
must assign a probability to
k
that is at most
\varepsilon
times the probability it assigns to
\ell
, see
[
Sel75
]
.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Hansen
[
Han19
]
via a reduction from Minmax value
(GT7) Minmax Value
.

(GT10) Strategy Sets Closed under Rational Behavior

Given:

A 3-player game
\Gamma
in strategic form, and a collection of subsets
S_{i}\subseteq\Sigma_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
.

Question:

Is the collection
\{S_{i}\}
closed under rational behavior (CURB)? Here sets of pure strategies

\{S_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
of an
m
-player game is CURB if and only if for all pure strategies
s
of Player
i
such that
s
is a best reply to a distribution
S_{1}\times S_{2}\times\ldots\times S_{i-1}\times S_{i+1}\times\ldots\times S_{m}
,
we have that
s\in S_{i}
.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Hansen
[
Han19
]
via a reduction from Minmax value
(GT7) Minmax Value
.

(GT11) Pareto Optimal Nash Equilibrium

Given:

A
3
-player game
\Gamma
in strategic form.

Question:

Does
\Gamma
have a Pareto optimal Nash equilibrium? A collection
\mathbf{\sigma}
of mixed strategies is Pareto optimal if there does not exist any
\mathbf{\sigma}^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma}^{\prime})\geq\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma})
for all
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, and
\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma}^{\prime})>\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma})
for at least one
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Berthelsen, Hansen
[
BH22
]
via a reduction from the variant of Nash Equilibrium with Bounded Payoffs
(GT2) Nash Equilibrium with Bounded Payoffs
, in which we require
\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma})=0
.

Comments:

The result also holds for zero-sum games. The problem of deciding whether there exists a non-Pareto optimal Nash equilibrium is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard for symmetric win-lose games as well
[
BM21
,
BHM23
]
.

Open Questions:

It is open whether the result also holds for win-lose games
[
BHM23
]
.

(GT12) Strong Nash Equilibrium

Given:

A
3
-player game
\Gamma
in strategic form.

Question:

Does
\Gamma
have a strong Nash equilibrium? A collection
\mathbf{\sigma}
of mixed strategies is a strong Nash equilibrium if no coalition of players can together increase their payoff, hence if there is no nonempty subset
B\subseteq\{1,2,3\}
such that all players in
B
can increase their payoff by simultaneously deviating from their strategies in
\mathbf{\sigma}
.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Berthelsen, Hansen
[
BH22
]
via a reduction from the variant of Nash Equilibrium with Bounded Payoffs
(GT2) Nash Equilibrium with Bounded Payoffs
in which we require
\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma})=0
.

Comments:

The result also holds for zero-sum games. The problem of deciding whether there exists a non-strong Nash equilibrium is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard for symmetric win-lose games as well
[
BHM23
]
.

Open Questions:

It is open whether the result also holds for win-lose games
[
BHM23
]
.

(GT13) Nonsymmetric Nash Equilibrium

Given:

A symmetric
3
-player game
\Gamma
in strategic form.

Question:

Does
\Gamma
have a nonsymmetric Nash equilibrium?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Berthelsen, Hansen
[
BH22
]
via a reduction from the variant of Nash Equilibrium with Bounded Payoffs
(GT2) Nash Equilibrium with Bounded Payoffs
in which we require
\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma})=0
.

(GT14) Irrational Nash Equilibrium

Given:

A
3
-player game
\Gamma
in strategic form.

Question:

Does
\Gamma
have a Nash equilibrium
\mathbf{\sigma}
in which
\sigma_{i}(s)\not\in\mathbb{Q}
for some
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and some
s\in\Sigma_{i}
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard by Berthelsen, Hansen
[
BH22
]
via a reduction from the variant of Nash Equilibrium with Bounded Payoffs
(GT2) Nash Equilibrium with Bounded Payoffs
in which we require
\mathsf{U}_{i}(\mathbf{\sigma})=0
.

Comments:

The result also holds for zero-sum games
[
BH22
]
, and for win-lose games
[
BHM23
]
.
The problem of deciding whether there exists a rational Nash equilibrium, in which
\sigma_{i}(s)\in\mathbb{Q}
for all
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and all
s\in\Sigma_{i}
is shown to be
\exists\mathbb{Q}
-hard
[
BH22
]
. It is open whether the problem is even decidable.

17.2
Games in Extensive Form

Games in
extensive form
are defined by a rooted tree with utility vectors at the leaves. Nodes of the tree are controlled either by one of the player, or by a so-called chance player, in which case we associate a probability distribution to the outgoing edges. In games in extensive form with
imperfect information
, strategies can only be based on partial information, or
signals
regarding the moves of the opponent.
A behavioral strategy is a mixed strategy based only on the received signals.
In games with
imperfect recall
, not only strategies can only be based on partial information, but players can forget about the sequence of signals or actions.
The definitions extend to games that are defined on directed acyclic graphs.
We refer to Hansen
[
Han19
]
and Gimbert, Paul, Srivathsan
[
GPS20
]
for detailed definitions.

(GT15) Trembling Hand Perfect Equilibrium for Games in Extensive Form

Given:

A 3-player game
\Gamma
in extensive form, and a pure Nash equilibrium
\mathbf{\sigma}
of
\Gamma
.

Question:

Is
\mathbf{\sigma}
a trembling hand perfect equilibrium?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Hansen
[
Han19
]
via a reduction from Minmax value
(GT7) Minmax Value
.

Comments:

We refer
[
Sel75
,
vD84
]
for the definition of trembling hand perfect equilibria for games in extensive form. Hansen
[
Han19
]
also gives
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness results for other properties of pure Nash equilibria of games in extensive form, in particular that of belonging to a
sequential equilibrium

[
KW82
]
and being a
quasi-perfect equilibrium

[
vD84
]
.

Also see:

Trembling Hand Perfect Equilibrium
(GT8) Trembling Hand Perfect Equilibrium
.

(GT16) Non-negative Expected Payoff for Games in Extensive Form

Given:

A one-player game in extensive form, with imperfect recall.

Question:

Is the maximum expected payoff, over all behavioral strategies, non-negative?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Gimbert, Paul, Srivathsan
[
GPS20
]
, via a reduction from feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
. The result also holds for deciding whether the maximum payoff is strictly positive.

Also see:

Feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
.

(GT17) Nonnegative Maxmin Payoff for Games in Extensive Form

Given:

A zero-sum two-player game in extensive form, with imperfect recall.

Question:

Is the maxmin payoff, over all behavioral strategies, non-negative?

Complexity:

Both
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard and
\forall\mathbb{R}
-hard, and belongs to
\exists\forall\mathbb{R}
by Gimbert, Paul, Srivathsan
[
GPS20
]
. Reductions are from Non-negative Expected Payoff for Games in Extensive form
(GT16) Non-negative Expected Payoff for Games in Extensive Form
.

Also see:

Non-negative Expected Payoff for Games in Extensive form
(GT16) Non-negative Expected Payoff for Games in Extensive Form
.

(GT18) Stationary Nash Equilibrium in Acyclic Games

Given:

An acyclic
m
-player recursive game with payoff demands
L\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Question:

Does there exist a stationary Nash equilibrium satisfying all the demands? A stationary equilibrium is one involving stationary strategies, that only depend on the current node of the graph.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Hansen and Sølvsten
[
HS20
]
. The reduction is from feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
with homogeneous quadratic polynomials over a simplex.

Comments:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness holds even for
7
-player games with non-negative rewards.

17.3
Fair Division Problems

(GT19) Square-Cut Pizza Sharing

Given:

A collection of
n
pizzas, where a
pizza
is a mass distribution (measure) on
[0,1]^{2}
that can be computed for polygonal subsets using arithmetic circuits.

Question:

Can all pizzas be halved simultaneously by a
square cut
, that is, a single cut which is a polygonal arc consisting of horizontal and vertical segments, and such that the square cut makes at most
n-2
turns?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Deligkas, Fearnley, and Melissourgos
[
DFM20
,
DFM22
]
via a reduction from consensus halving
(GT21) Consensus Halving
. Remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete even if each pizza consists of at most
6
square or triangular pieces of mass (not necessarily uniform), and each point belongs to at most
3
pizzas
[
DFM20
,
DFM22
]
.

Comments:

The
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hardness result is sharp in the sense that
n
pizzas can always be halved simultaneously with a square-cut making at most
n-1
turns
[
KRPS16
]
.

Also see:

Line-cut pizza sharing
(GT20) Line-Cut Pizza Sharing
, consensus halving
(GT21) Consensus Halving
.

(GT20) Line-Cut Pizza Sharing

Given:

A collection of
2n
pizzas, where a
pizza
is a mass distribution (measure) on
[0,1]^{2}
that can be computed for polygonal subsets using arithmetic circuits.

Question:

Can all pizzas be halved simultaneously by making
k
line cuts (and assembling the resulting pieces appropriately)?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Schnider
[
Sch21c
]
via a reduction from consensus halving
(GT21) Consensus Halving
with
k=n-1

Comments:

The
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hardness result is sharp in the sense that
2n
pizzas can always be halved simultaneously with at most
n
line cuts
[
HK20
]
.

Also see:

Square-cut pizza sharing
(GT19) Square-Cut Pizza Sharing
, consensus halving
(GT21) Consensus Halving
.

(GT21) Consensus Halving

Given:

A family of
n
arithmetic circuits computing functions
F_{i}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
i\in[n]
, a natural number
k\in\mathbb{N}
. The functions
F_{i}
are used to valuate intervals and unions of intervals by defining
F_{i}([a,b])=F_{i}(b)-F_{i}(a)
, and
F_{i}(A\cup B)=F_{i}(A)+F_{i}(B)
for disjoint subsets
A
and
B
of
[0,1]
.

Question:

Is there a
k
-cut
, that is,
k
points
0\leq x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{k}\leq 1
, with the following property: if we split
[0,1]
into
two sets
A_{+}=\bigcup_{0\leq 2i\leq k}[x_{2i},x_{2i+1}]
and
A_{-}=\bigcup_{0\leq 2i+1\leq k}[x_{2i+1},x_{2i+2}]
, where we let
x_{0}=0
and
x_{k+1}=1
, then
F_{i}(A_{+})=F_{i}(A_{-})
for all
i\in[n]
? That is all agents, the holders of the valuation functions, valuate the two “halves”
A_{+}
and
A_{-}
equally.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Deligkas, Fearnley, Melissourgos, and Spirakis
[
DFMS22
]
via a reduction from feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
over a compact domain. The problem is hard for
k=n-1
and always solvable (using the Borsuk-Ulam Theorem) for
k=n
.

Open Questions:

Does the problem remain hard if the
F_{i}
are given as polynomials?

Also see:

Square-cut pizza sharing
(GT19) Square-Cut Pizza Sharing
, line-cut pizza sharing
(GT20) Line-Cut Pizza Sharing
.

17.4
Markets

(GT22) Equilibrium in Exchange Markets

Given:

An Arrow-Debreu exchange market, consisting of a set
\mathcal{G}
of
g
divisible goods, a set
\mathcal{A}
of agents, a matrix
W
such that
W_{ij}
is the amount of good
j
with agent
i
, and a non-negative piecewise-linear concave utility function
U_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{g}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Question:

Is there an equilibrium in the market, that is,
p\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{g}
and
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{g}
for all
i\in\mathcal{A}
, such that

\displaystyle\forall i\in\mathcal{A}:x_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{arg}\max_{y}\{U_{i}(y):\sum_{j}y_{j}p_{j}\leq\sum_{j}W_{ij}p_{j}\}

\displaystyle\forall j\in\mathcal{G}:\sum_{i}x_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}W_{ij}?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Garg, Mehta, Vazirani, and Yazdanbod
[
GMVY17
]
via a reduction from feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
.
The result holds even under the assumption that the utility functions are Leontief utility functions, a special subclass of piecewise-linear concave functions, see
[
GMVY17
]
for definitions.

18
Machine Learning and Probabilistic Reasoning

Artificial neural networks come in many variants. For the purpose of this section,
we only define fully-connected, feed-forward, ReLU networks.
Such a network realizes a parameterized function
N_{p}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
which maps an input
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
to some output
N_{p}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
given some parameters
p
(weights and biases).
Given a set of data points
(x,y)
, the goal is to find the right
p
such that
N_{p}
predicts the
y
-values of the data points to within a given threshold.
The function
N_{p}
is the composition of two types of functions.
The first type is a simple affine transformation
x\mapsto Ax+b
.
The second type is an activation function; examples include the identity function and the rectified linear unit activation function
ReLU
.
(The name is more complicated than the function itself.)

ReLU
maps
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
y_{i}=\max(x_{i},0)
.
An artificial neural network can be described as a repeated composition
of affine functions and
ReLU
’s.
We refer the interested reader to the comprehensive book by Goodfellow, Bengio and Aaron on Deep learning
[
GBC16
]
, for an in-depth introduction of the great variety neural network architectures and training methods.

When we want to reason about probabilities in a formal sense, we can define
formal languages
\mathcal{L}
which can express probabilities of events,
where events can again be combined using elementary variables, conditional probabilities and conjunction of events, comparison of probabilities, independence statements, confirmation statements and potentially many more.
There is an illustrative example that shows us that irrational numbers might be needed to find a solution.
We try to find probabilities such that
\Pr(A\cap B)=1-\Pr(A\cap B)
and

\Pr(A\cap B)=\Pr(B)\cdot\Pr(B)

implies that
\Pr(B)=\sqrt{1/2}
.

(ML1) Training Neural Networks

Given:

A set of data points, a neural network architecture, a cost function, a threshold

Question:

Do there exist biases and weights such that the total error is below the threshold?

Complexity:

First Abrahamsen, Kleist and Miltzow
[
AKM21
]
showed
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness for arbitrary network architectures, two layers, and the identity activation function.
Bertschinger, Hertrich, Jungeblut, Miltzow, and Weber
[
BHJ+22
]
strengthened the result to fully-connected neural networks with only two input, two output neurons and the ReLU activation function.
In case that we allow that some weights are given in the input than the problem was shown to be
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete already in 1992 by Zhang
[
Zha92
]
.

Comments:

The
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hardness results were extended in part to other activation functions involving non-algebraic functions
[
HHKV23
]
.
In that case the problems might not be even decidable.
To make the problems with non-algebraic activation functions easier, the neural network problem was studied in a regime where incorrect answers are allowed
[
BDC23
]
.

Also see:

Reachability in neural network
(ML2) Reachability in Neural Networks
.

(ML2) Reachability in Neural Networks

Given:

A feedforward neural network
N
with weights and biases, seen as a function from
N:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and two convex polytopes
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
, described by linear inequalities.

Question:

Do there exist points
x\in A
and
y\in B
such that
N(x)=y
?

Complexity:

The complexity of the problem depends on the type of activation functions that are allowed, see Wurm
[
Wur23
]
.
The problem becomes
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard for many common non-linear activation functions.

Comments:

Wurm
[
Wur23
]
shows a close relation to constraint satisfaction problems (CSPs).
Different computational complexities of CSPs translate directly to the corresponding complexity of the neural network reachability problem.

Also see:

Training neural network
(ML1) Training Neural Networks
.

(ML3) Satisfiability of Probabilistic Languages
\mathcal{L}

Given:

A formula
\varphi
in the probabilistic language
\mathcal{L}
.

Question:

Does there exists an assignment of probabilities that makes
\varphi
true?

Complexity:

Mossé, Ibeling, and Icard
[
MII21
]
showed that the problem is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for the languages

\mathcal{L}^{\textrm{cond}}_{\textrm{prob}}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\textrm{poly}}_{\textrm{prob}}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\textrm{cond}}_{\textrm{causal}}
, and
\mathcal{L}^{\textrm{poly}}_{\textrm{causal}}
.
Ibeling, Icard, Mierzewski, and Mossé
[
IIMM23
]
showed
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness
for the languages

\mathcal{L}^{\textrm{ind}}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\textrm{confirm}}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\textrm{cond}}
, and

\mathcal{L}^{\textrm{poly}}

The problem is known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-complete for the languages

\mathcal{L}^{\textrm{comb}}_{\textrm{prob}}
,

\mathcal{L}^{\textrm{lin}}_{\textrm{prob}}
,

\mathcal{L}^{\textrm{comp}}_{\textrm{causal}}
, and
or
\mathcal{L}^{\textrm{lin}}_{\textrm{causal}}
, see also Fagin
[
FHM90
]
.
The reductions are from
ETR-INV

(L5) ETR-INV
.

Comments:

The results indicate that the difference between

\mathsf{NP}
-completeness and
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness is caused by
allowing conditional probability statements
or the multiplication of probabilities.
The satisfiability problem for probabilistic languages that are allowed to contain summation symbols, e.g. abbreviating a sum like
1+2+3+...+n
as
\sum_{i=1}^{n}i
, is complete for the class succ
\exists\mathbb{R}
, a real version of
\mathsf{NEXP}

[
vdZBL23
]
.

Also see:

ETR-INV

(L5) ETR-INV
.

(ML4) Gaussian Conditional Independence Inference Problem

Given:

A Conditional Independence Inference Rule
\varphi
. See
[
Stu05
]
for definitions.

Question:

Is the rule
\varphi
valid for all regular Gaussians?

Complexity:

Boege (see
[
Boe22a
, Theorem 5.38]
and
[
Boe21
, Theorem 11]
) showed that the problem is
\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete.

Comments:

The proof is via a reduction from feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
, using Shor’s normal and an encdoing via geometric von Staudt gadgets.

Also see:

Feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
.

19
Markov Chains and Decision Processes

We recommend the book by Sutton and Barto on reinforcement learning
[
SB18
]
for the definition of Markov Decision Processes (MDPs) and their variants, to which a full chapter is dedicated, and which does an excellent job of illustrating the notion in-depth.

(MDP1) Realizability of polynomial parametric Markov Decision Processes

Given:

A polynomial, parametric Markov Decision Process with at least two states.

Question:

Is the polynomial parametric Markov Decision Process realizable?

Complexity:

The problem is first claimed to be
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Junges, Katoen, Pérez, and Winkler
[
JKPW21
]
via a reduction from local positivity over a bounded domain
(A2) (Strict) Local Positivity
.
They in turn attribute this to earlier work by
Lanotte, Maggiolo-Schettini, Troina
[
LMT07
]
.

Comments:

Polynomial and parametric in the context of Markov Decision Processes mean that the transition probabilities are polynomial functions
f(x)
instead of real numbers.
The question is whether there exists some
x
such that
f
describes a well-defined Markov Decision Process.

(MDP2) Never Worse States in Graph Parametric MDP

Given:

A (non-weighted) parametric Markov Decision Process
\mathcal{M}
with graph
G(\mathcal{M})=(V,E)
, together with
v\in V
and
W\subseteq V
.

Question:

Is
v
never worse than
W
?

Complexity:

Engelen, Pérez, and Rao showed that the problem is
\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
EPR23
]
via a reduction from (the complement of) strict local positivity
(A2) (Strict) Local Positivity
.

Also see:

Equally bad states in graph parameteric MDP
(MDP3) Equally Bad States in Graph Parametric MDP
, strict local positivity
(A2) (Strict) Local Positivity
.

(MDP3) Equally Bad States in Graph Parametric MDP

Given:

A (non-weighted) parametric Markov Decision Process
\mathcal{M}
with graph
G(\mathcal{M})=(V,E)
, together with
v,w\in V
.

Question:

Is
v
never worse than
w
and vice versa?

Complexity:

Engelen, Pérez, and Rao showed that the problem is
\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
EPR23
]
via a reduction from
(MDP2) Never Worse States in Graph Parametric MDP
.

Also see:

Never worse states in graph parameteric MDP
(MDP2) Never Worse States in Graph Parametric MDP
.

(MDP4) Total Variation Distance in MDP

Given:

A (labeled) Markov Decision Process and two initial distributions
\mu
and
\nu
.

Question:

Is there a (memoryless) strategy such that total variation distance in the induced Markov Chain equals
0
?

Complexity:

Kiefer and Tang showed that the problem is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
KT20
]
via a reduction from non-negative matrix factorization
(A21) Non-negative Matrix Factorization (NMF)
. Remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for total variation distance less than
1

[
KT20
]
.

Comments:

The problem is polynomial time decidable if we ask if the total variation distance can be made larger than
0
.
For the related notion of probabilistic bisimilarity all studied cases are either
\mathsf{NP}
-complete or in
\mathsf{P}

[
KT20
]
.

Open Questions:

If we ask for total variation distance equal to one then it is known that the problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard and in
\exists\mathbb{R}

[
KT20
]
.
So it remains to settle this case.

Also see:

Non-negative matrix factorization
(A21) Non-negative Matrix Factorization (NMF)
.

(MDP5) Reachability for Augmented Interval Markov Chain

Given:

An augmented interval Markov Chain
\mathcal{M}
, an initial vertex
s
, a target vertex
t
, and a threshold
\tau\in[0,1]
.

Question:

Does there exists an Markov Chain
M\in[\mathcal{M}]
refining
\mathcal{M}
such that
the probability to go from
s
to
t
is at least/most
\tau
?

Complexity:

Chonev showed that the problem is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
Cho19
]
.

Comments:

The problem is also
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, if we consider the approximate version.

Also see:

Quantitative reachabilty for parametric acyclic Markov chain
(MDP7) Quantitative Reachability for Parametric Acyclic Markov Chains
.

(MDP6) Model Checking for Interval Markov Chains w.r.t. Unambiguous Automata

Given:

An interval Markov chain and a regular language
E
.

Question:

Does there exists values for the undetermined transitions of the Markov chain such that
E
is satisfied?

Complexity:

The problem was shown to be
{\mathsf{NP}}_{\mathbb{R}}
-complete by Benedikt, Lenhardt, and Worrell
[
BLW13a
]
.
The same proof easily also shows
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness.

Comments:

They reduce from Local Positivity
(A2) (Strict) Local Positivity
on a bounded domain with a single multivariate polynomial of a specific form.

(MDP7) Quantitative Reachability for Parametric Acyclic Markov Chains

Given:

A parametric
acyclic
Markov Chain and a target state
T
.

Question:

Does there exists a well-defined and graph preserving valuation of the probability transitions such that the probability to reach
T
is at least/most
1/2
?

Complexity:

The problem was shown to be
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Junges, Katoen, Pérez, and Winkler
[
JKPW21
]
.

Comments:

The problem is very reminiscent to the Reachability problem for Augmented Interval Markov Chains.
Clearly, the
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness also carries over to Markov Decision Processes.
The authors also use the term
parameter synthesis
in this context to describe the question of finding parameters for some property to hold.

Also see:

Reachabilty for augmented interval Markov chain
(MDP5) Reachability for Augmented Interval Markov Chain
.

(MDP8) Memoryless Distance Minimisation Problem for Markov Decision Processes

Given:

A Markov Decision Process with two initial states, and a threshold.

Question:

Does there exists a memoryless strategy such that the distance between the two initial states is at most the threshold?

Complexity:

The problem was shown to be
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Kiefer and Tang
[
KT24
]
.

Comments:

They reduce from strict local Positivity
(A2) (Strict) Local Positivity
, with bounded domain and a single polynomial of degree
6
.

20
Miscellaneous

In this section, we collect problems that we could not fit into any
of the other categories.

(Misc1) Euclidean Preference

Given:

A finite set
A
, and a
profile

V
, a collection of strict orders
\succ_{v}
over
A
, that is, each
\succ_{v}
is total, irreflexive, antisymmetric and transitive. The orders are also called
preference relations
or
votes
.

Question:

Is there a function
x:V\cup A\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
a\succ b
implies that
\lVert x(v)-x(a)\rVert_{2}<\lVert x(v)-x(b)\rVert_{2}

for all
v\in V
and
a,b\in A
? In that case the profile
V
is known as
d
-Euclidean
.

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for any fixed
d\geq 2
by Peters
[
Pet17
]
via a reduction from oriented hyperplane realizability
(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in

R
d

. Remains
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if all orders are
dichotomous
, that is, there is no triple
a,b,c
with
a\succ_{v}b\succ_{v}c
for any
v\in V

[
Pet16
,
Pet17
]
.

Comments:

For the Manhattan and maximum metrics the problem lies in
\mathsf{NP}
, so it is unlikely to be
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard in those cases
[
Pet17
]
;
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness seems to be open for those metrics.

Also see:

Oriented hyperplane realizability
(CG13) Oriented Hyperplane Realizability in

R
d

.

(Misc2) (Ultimate) Positivity of Linear Recurrence

Given:

Coefficients
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k}
and initial values
u_{0},\ldots,u_{k-1}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k}
.
With these numbers we define the sequence
u_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}u_{n-i}
for
n\geq k
.

Question:

Is the sequence
(u_{n})_{n\geq 0}

(ultimately) positive
; that is, is
u_{n}>0
for (almost) all
n
?

Complexity:

Both positivity and ultimate positivity are
\forall\mathbb{R}
-hard by Ouaknine and Worrell
[
OW17
]
via a reduction from strict local positivity (of a polynomial)
(A2) (Strict) Local Positivity
. For simple recurrences (the characteristic polynomial has no multiple roots), the problems can be decided in
{\mathsf{coNP}}^{\exists\mathbb{R}}

[
OW14
,
OW17
]
.

Comments:

These problems are related to Skolem’s problem which asks whether
u_{n}=0
for some
n
. Decidability of Skolem’s problem is a famously open question.

Open Questions:

Does positivity (or ultimate positivity) lie in
\forall\mathbb{R}
?
\blacktriangledown
What is the complexity of the robust version of the problem (in which each
a_{i}
is given with a small neighborhood), as studied in
[
Vah23
]
?

Also see:

Strict local positivity
(A2) (Strict) Local Positivity
, zeroness of convolution recursive sequence
(A-Open12) Zeroness of Convolution Recursive Sequence
.

(Misc3) Linear Update Loop Termination

Given:

Boolean formula
\varphi(x)
over polynomial inequalities in
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, vector
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Question:

Does the program
\mathrm{\mathbf{while}}\ \varphi(x)\ \mathrm{\mathbf{do}}\ x=Ax+b
terminate for every initial value
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
?

Complexity:

\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete by Frohn, Hark, Giesl
[
FHG23
, Theorem 7.5]
via a reduction from feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
. Remains
\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete if
b=0
, but becomes
\mathsf{coNP}
-complete if
\varphi
consists of linear polynomial conditions only
[
FHG23
, Theorem 7.3]
.

Comments:

The non-trivial part in this result is
\forall\mathbb{R}
-membership, the
\forall\mathbb{R}
-hardness is immediate with program
\mathrm{\mathbf{while}}\ \varphi(x)\ \mathrm{\mathbf{do}}\ x=x
. For earlier work on related problems, see
[
COW15
]
.

Open Questions:

What is the complexity of the problem if instead we ask whether the program terminates for a specific initial value
x\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}
? For specific types of conditions
\varphi(x)
this was called the Extended Orbit Problem in
[
COW15
]
and shown to lie in
\exists\mathbb{R}
. Another problem that has similarities is the compact escape problem
(A42) Compact Escape Problem
.

Also see:

(Ultimate) positivity of linear recurrence
(Misc2) (Ultimate) Positivity of Linear Recurrence
, compact escape problem
(A42) Compact Escape Problem
, feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
.

21
Higher Levels of the Real Hierarchy

21.1
Logic

(L17)
Q_{1}\cdots Q_{k}
-Fragment of the Theory of the Reals

Given:

A sentence over the signature
(0,1,+,\cdot)
with alternating quantifier blocks
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k}
, with
Q_{i}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
comparison operators
\{=,<\leq,\geq,>\}
and logic connectives
\vee,\wedge
and
\neg
.

Question:

Is the sentence true if interpreted over the reals?

Complexity:

Complete for
Q_{1}\cdots Q_{k}\mathbb{R}
by Bürgisser and Cucker
[
BC09
]
. The problem remains complete for

Q_{1}\cdots Q_{k}\mathbb{R}
even if the matrix is of the form
f(x)>0
or
f(x)\geq 0
, for a polynomial
f
, and, under certain conditions, for
f(x)=0
, and if the quantifiers are restricted to
(-1,1)
or
[-1,1]
, as shown by Schaefer and Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
, Tables 1 and 2]
. There are also some robustness results, mostly on the first and second level, that allow the use of exotic quantifiers,
\exists^{*},\forall^{*}
and
H
, see
[
BC09
,
Jun23b
]
.

Also see:

ETR

(L1) Existential Theory of the Reals (ETR)
.

21.2
Algebra

(A42) Compact Escape Problem

Given:

A compact semialgebraic set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, a matrix
A\in\mathbb{Q}^{n\times n}
.

Question:

For all
x\in K
is there a
k
such that
A^{k}x\not\in K
?

Complexity:

\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by D’Costa, Lefaucheux, Neumann, Ouaknine, and Worrell
[
DLN+21
]
via a reduction from the
\forall\exists
-fragment of the theory of the reals
(L17)

Q

1

⋯
Q

k

-Fragment of the Theory of the Reals
.

Comments:

D’Costa, Lefaucheux, Neumann, Ouaknine, and Worrell
[
DLN+21
]
show that the problem is hard for
\forall\exists_{<}\mathbb{R}
, hardness then follows, since
\forall\exists\mathbb{R}=\forall\exists_{<}\mathbb{R}
by Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
.

Also see:

Q_{1}\cdots Q_{k}
-fragment of the theory of the reals
(L17)

Q

1

⋯
Q

k

-Fragment of the Theory of the Reals
, linear update loop termination
(Misc3) Linear Update Loop Termination
.

(A43) Hausdorff Distance

Given:

Two semialgebraic sets
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
d\in\mathbb{Q}
. The
Hausdorff distance
is defined as
d_{H}(A,B):=\max(\sup_{a\in A}\inf_{b\in B}\lVert a-b\rVert,\sup_{b\in B}\inf_{a\in A}\lVert a-b\rVert)
.

Question:

Do
A
and
B
have Hausdorff distance at most
d
, that is,
is
d_{H}(A,B)\leq d
?

Complexity:

\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Jungeblut, Kleist, Miltzow
[
JKM23
]
and Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
via reductions from the
\forall\exists
-fragment of the theory of the reals
(L17)

Q

1

⋯
Q

k

-Fragment of the Theory of the Reals
. The problem remains hard for
d=0

[
JKM23
,
SŠ23
]
or
d
double-exponentially small
[
JKM23
]
.

Comments:

Jungeblut, Kleist, Miltzow
[
JKM23
]
show that the problem is complete for
\forall\exists_{<}\mathbb{R}
, Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
show the full result, based on Bürgisser, Cucker
[
BC09
]
who called the
d=0
case
Euclidean Relative Denseness
.. The directed Hausdorff distance,
\sup_{a\in A}\inf_{b\in B}\lVert a-b\rVert
is also
\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
JKM23
,
SŠ23
]
. The proofs are

Open Questions:

Jungeblut asks whether the problem becomes
\exists\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if we ask whether there is a translation
t
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
d_{H}(t(A),B)\leq d
; this is a restricted case of the Gromov-Hausdorff distance
(A-Open6) Gromov-Hausdorff Distance in Euclidean Space
.

Also see:

Distance of semialgebraic sets
(A40) Distance of Semialgebraic Sets
, Gromov-Hausdorff distance
(A-Open6) Gromov-Hausdorff Distance in Euclidean Space
, Fréchet distance of surfaces
(CG-Open29) Fréchet Distance of Surfaces
.

(A44) Surjectivity

Given:

A polynomial
f
in
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
, domain
A
, codomain
B
.

Question:

Is
f
surjective as a function from
A
to
B
?

Complexity:

\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
extending work by Bürgisser and Cucker
[
BC09
, Proposition 4.1]
. Hardness can be obtained via a reduction from a constrained version of the
Q_{1}\cdots Q_{k}
-fragment of the theory of the reals
(L17)

Q

1

⋯
Q

k

-Fragment of the Theory of the Reals
. The problem remains
\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if
A
and
B
are Cartesian products of
\mathbb{R}
and
[-1,1]
and the total degree of
f
is bounded.

Comments:

Bürgisser and Cucker
[
BC09
]
showed that surjectivity of rational functions given by arithmetic circuits is
\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, Schaefer and Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
proved the result for polynomials.

Open Questions:

Does the problem remain
\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if both
A
and
B
are of the form
\mathbb{R}^{n}
?

Also see:

Image density (arithmetic circuit)
(A45) Image Density (arithmetic circuit)
.

(A45) Image Density (arithmetic circuit)

Given:

An arithmetic circuit (with sign function), defining a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

Question:

Is the range of
f
dense in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
?

Complexity:

\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, combining results from Bürgisser, Cucker
[
BC09
, Proposition 5.7, Corollary 9.4]
and Junginger
[
Jun23b
, Theorem 4.27]
.

Comments:

Bürgisser and Cucker show that this problem is hard for a complexity class involving exotic quantifiers; Junginger shows that that class equals
\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
. In the Zariski topology, Bürgisser, Cucker
[
BC09
, Proposition 5.7, Corollary 9.4]
show that the problem is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete.

Also see:

Surjectivity (second level)
(A44) Surjectivity
, domain density (arithmetic circuit)
(A13) Domain Density (arithmetic circuit)
, density of semialgebraic set
(A34) Radius of Semialgebraic Set
.

21.3
Computational Geometry

(CG75) Area Universality For Triples with Partial Area Assignment

Given:

Vertices
V
, a set of vertex triples
F\subseteq\binom{V}{3}
, and a
partial area assignment
, that is, a function
\alpha^{\prime}:F^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, where
F^{\prime}\subseteq F
.

Question:

An
area assignment
assigns a positive real number to every triple in
F
. Can every area assignment
\alpha
that extends
\alpha^{\prime}
, that is, agrees with it on
F^{\prime}
, be realized by placing the vertices of
V
in the plane so that every vertex triple in
F
forms a triangle with the area prescribed by
\alpha
?

Complexity:

\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete by Dobbins, Kleist, Miltzow, and Rzążewski
[
DKMR23
]
via a reduction from a constrained form of the
\forall\exists
-fragment of the theory of the reals
(L17)

Q

1

⋯
Q

k

-Fragment of the Theory of the Reals
. The problem remains hard if the size of
F
is linear in the size of
V

Comments:

This problem is a step towards settling the complexity of the area universality problem
(CG-Open33) Area Universality
. Dobbins, Kleist, Miltzow, and Rzążewski
[
DKMR23
]
suggest studying triangulations in a next step.

Also see:

Area Universality
(CG-Open33) Area Universality
, prescribed area extension
(CG60) Prescribed Area Extension
.

22
Candidates

22.1
Logic

(L-Open1) Universality of Unambiguous Context-Free Grammar (UUCFG)

Given:

A context-free grammar
G
over alphabet
\Sigma
which is
unambiguous
, that is, every word generated by the grammar has a unique derivation tree.

Question:

Is the grammar
universal
, that is, is
L(G)=\Sigma^{*}
?

Complexity:

This problem was shown to lie in
\forall\mathbb{R}
by Clemente
[
Cle20
]
using another interesting intermediate problem, zeroness of convolution recursive sequences
(A-Open12) Zeroness of Convolution Recursive Sequence
, which also lies in
\forall\mathbb{R}
. Clemente also mentions a more direct proof of membership in Stanisław Purgał’s master’s thesis. Finally, he shows that a slightly generalized problem, the coin-flip measure comparison problem, is as hard as
SSQR
problem.

Also see:

Positivity of linear recurrence
(Misc2) (Ultimate) Positivity of Linear Recurrence
, zeroness of convolution recursive sequence
(A-Open12) Zeroness of Convolution Recursive Sequence
.

(L-Open2) Existential Theory of Invertible Matrices

Given:

A conjunction of conditions of the form
AX_{i}=X_{j}B
, where the
X_{k}
are (matrix) variables and
A,B\in\mathbb{Q}^{n\times n}
.

Question:

Are there invertible matrices
X_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
satisfying all conditions?

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
.

Comments:

The problem was introduced by Dubut
[
Dub20
]
to upper-bound the complexity of the bisimilarity of diagrams (itself a possible candidate for
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness?). Without the invertibility contraints, the problem turns into a linear program becoming solvable in polynomial time. It may be easier to show the problem
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete if one is given access to the inverses of the
X_{i}
, but that’s not the case in the original problem.

Also see:

Matrix singularity
(A15) Matrix Singularity
, constrained matrix invertibility problem
(A-Open7) Constrained Matrix Invertibility Problem
, feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
.

22.2
Algebra

(A-Open1) Feasibility of Univariate Polynomial

Given:

A univariate polynomial
f\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
defined by a straight-line program or arithmetic circuit.

Question:

Does
f
have a root, that is, is there an
x
such that
f(x)=0
?

Complexity:

Known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard (for a history of proofs, see Perrucci, Sabia
[
PS07
]
), and in
\exists\mathbb{R}
. Is it
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete?

Comments:

This candidate is a bit surprising; the known
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness proofs are quite intricate. Does the problem lie in
\mathsf{NP}
?
If one asks whether
f
has a
rational
root in a given interval, then the problem is decidable using Eisenstein’s criterion, see for example
[
DGVVG99
, Lemma 5.22]
; that makes it somewhat less likely that the rational version of the problem is
\exists\mathbb{Q}
-hard. See
[
BJ24
]
for complexity results on counting the number of real roots.

Open Questions:

What is the computational complexity of determining whether
f
has a rational root in some given interval?

Also see:

Feasibility
(A1) Feasibility
.

(A-Open2) Polynomial Equivalence under Shifts

Given:

Two polynomials
f,g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
in
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}]
.

Question:

Is there a vector
b
such that
f(x)=g(x+b)
?

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
.

Comments:

This problem is discussed in Chillara, Grichener, and Shpilka
[
CGS23
]
, but the authors switch to a minimality version: given a polynomial
f
, is there a vector
b
such that
f(x+b)
has fewer monomials? For this version the authors show that over an integral domain which is not a field, the minimality version is as hard as the existential theory of the domain. Over
\mathbb{Z}
this implies that the problem is undecidable, but the reduction does not apply to
\mathbb{R}
(or
\mathbb{Q}
or
\mathbb{C}
).

Also see:

Polynomial projection (linear)
(A14) Polynomial Projection (Linear)
.

(A-Open3) Semialgebraic Set with Isolated Point

Given:

A semialgebraic set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Question:

Does
S
contain an isolated point; that is, is there an
x\in S
such that
S
contains no other points close to
x
?

Complexity:

In
\exists\forall\mathbb{R}
and known to be
\forall\mathbb{R}
-hard by Bürgisser, Cucker
[
BC09
, Corollary 6.8, Corollary 9.4]
, but not known to be
\exists\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete.

Comments:

Testing whether a specific point belonging to
S
is isolated, is
\forall\mathbb{R}
-complete, see the Isolated zero problem
(A7) Isolated Zero (ISO)
.

Also see:

Isolated point of semialgebraic set
(A38) Isolated Point of Semialgebraic Set
, isolated zero
(A7) Isolated Zero (ISO)
.

(A-Open4) Star-Shapedness of Semialgebraic Set

Given:

A semialgebraic set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Question:

Is
S

star-shaped
? That is, is there a point in
S
which can
see
all points in
S
in the sense that the line segment connecting the points belongs to
S
?

Complexity:

In
\exists\forall\mathbb{R}
by definition.

Comments:

Suggested in Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
.

Also see:

Convexity of semialgebraic set
(A32) Convexity of Semialgebraic Set
.

(A-Open5) Vapnik-Červonenkis Dimension of Family of Semialgebraic Sets

Given:

A family of semialgebraic sets
{\cal F}=\{\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\varphi(x,y)\}:y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\}
, integer
k
.

Question:

Does
{\cal F}
have Vapnik-Červonenkis Dimension at least
k
, that is, are there
k
points
P
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that for every subset
P^{\prime}\subseteq P
of those points, there is an
S\in{\cal F}
such that
P^{\prime}=S\cap P
?

Complexity:

In
\exists\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
; probably not complete for that class, since the universal quantifier is discrete, not real.

Comments:

Suggested in Schaefer, Štefankovič
[
SŠ23
]
. Over discrete sets this problem is complete for the third level of the polynomial-time hierarchy
[
Sch99
]
.

(A-Open6) Gromov-Hausdorff Distance in Euclidean Space

Given:

Two semialgebraic sets
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
d\in\mathbb{Q}
. The
Hausdorff distance
of two sets
A
and
B
is
d_{H}(A,B):=\max(\sup_{a\in A}\inf_{b\in B}\lVert a-b\rVert,\sup_{b\in B}\inf_{a\in A}\lVert a-b\rVert)
. The
Gromov-Hausdorff distance,
d_{GH}(A,B)
, of
A
and
B
in Euclidean space
is the infimum of
d_{H}(f(A),g(B))
over all isometries (distance-preserving functions)
f,g
from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Question:

Do
A
and
B
have Gromov-Hausdorff distance at most
d
, that is,
is
d_{GH}(A,B)\leq d
?

Complexity:

In
\exists\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
by definition, and
\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
-hardness follows from the
\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
-hardness of the Hausdorff distance
(A43) Hausdorff Distance
.

Comments:

The Gromov-Hausdorff distance has a more general definition than the one given here; the fact that for Euclidean spaces it is equivalent to the definition of
d_{GH}
above, was shown by Mémoli
[
Mém08
]
. To reduce the Hausdorff distance to the Gromov-Hausdorff distance, we can add a set of isolated points to the two sets that forces the isometries to be the identity.

Also see:

Hausdorff distance
(A43) Hausdorff Distance
.

(A-Open7) Constrained Matrix Invertibility Problem

Given:

Two matrices
A,B\in\{0,*\}^{n\times n}
.

Question:

Can the
*
-entries in
A
and
B
be replaced by real entries so that
AB=I
, where
I
is the
n
-dimensional identity matrix?

Complexity:

Over finite fields, the problem is known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-complete, by Bjerkevik, Botnan, and Kerber
[
BBK20
]
.

Comments:

This problem arises in the study of interleaving and stability of persistence modules
[
BBK20
]
, there may be further relevant open problems in that area.

Also see:

Matrix singularity
(A15) Matrix Singularity
, existential theory of invertible matrices
(L-Open2) Existential Theory of Invertible Matrices
.

(A-Open8) Matrix
\ell^{p}
-Low Rank Approximation

Given:

A matrix
A\in\mathbb{Q}^{m\times n}
, an integer
k\geq 0
,
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
.

Question:

Is there a matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
such that
\lVert A-B\rVert_{p}<\varepsilon
and
\operatorname{rank}(B)\leq k
? Here,
\lVert\rVert_{p}
denotes the
\ell^{p}
-norm
, which is
(\sum_{i\in[m],j\in[n]}|A_{i,j}-B_{i,j}|^{p})^{1/p}
for
p\geq 1
, and
|\{(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]:A_{i,j}\neq B_{i,j}
for
p=0
.

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
, and known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for
p\in\{0,1\}
even for
k=1

[
GV18
]
.

Also see:

Maximum/minimum matrix rank
(A16) Minimum/Maximum Matrix Rank
.

(A-Open9) Hadamard Decomposition

Given:

A matrix
M\in\mathbb{Q}^{m\times n}
, natural numbers
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
.

Question:

Are there matrices
M_{1},M_{2}
of rank
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
such that

M=M_{1}\odot M_{2}
, where
\odot
is the component-wise product, known as the
Hadamard product
, of two matrices?

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
as observed by Ciaperoni, Gionis, and Mannila
[
CGM24
]
who asked the question.

Also see:

non-negative matrix factorization
(A21) Non-negative Matrix Factorization (NMF)
.

(A-Open10) Tensor Eigenvector Approximation

A
3
-dimensonal tensor
T=(t_{ijk})\in\mathbb{Q}^{d\times d\times d}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}
,
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
.

Question:

Is there an
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
within distance
\varepsilon
of an eigenvector
x^{\prime}
of
T
for eigenvalue
\lambda
; that is,
\sum_{i,j}t_{i,j,k}x^{\prime}_{i}x^{\prime}_{j}=\lambda x^{\prime}_{k}
, and
\lVert x-x^{\prime}\rVert<\varepsilon
?

Complexity:

Known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard by Hillar, Lim
[
HL13
]
and in
\exists\mathbb{R}
by definition. Is it
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete?

Also see:

Tensor with eigenvalue
0

0

, tensor rank

.

(A-Open11) Tensor Singular Value

-dimensional tensor

.

\sigma
a singular value of

? (See Hillar, Lim

, Section 6]

Complexity:

Known to be

, by Hillar, Lim

and in
\exists\mathbb{R}
by definition. Is it
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete?

Comments:

It is also open whether testing if

, Theorem 1.10]
.

Also see:

(A27) Tensor with Eigenvalue
