(A-Open12) Zeroness of Convolution Recursive Sequence

Given:

A convolution recursive sequence
f
. A sequence
f:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
convolution recursive
if there is a family
g=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}):\mathbb{R}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}
of polynomials
g_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}]
and
k
sequences of real numbers
f_{1}=f,f_{2},\ldots,f_{k}
such that
\sigma f_{i}=g_{i}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})
, where the polynomials
g_{i}
are interpreted as follows: addition is addition of sequences and multiplication is convolution of sequences; the operator
\sigma
shifts the sequence by one position.

Question:

Is
f(n)=0
for all
n
?

Complexity:

This problem lies in
\forall\mathbb{R}
by Clemente
[
Cle20
]
who uses it to show that universality of an unambiguous context-free grammar
(L-Open1) Universality of Unambiguous Context-Free Grammar (UUCFG)
lies in
\forall\mathbb{R}
.

Also see:

Universality of unambiguous context-free grammar
(L-Open1) Universality of Unambiguous Context-Free Grammar (UUCFG)
, positivity of linear recurrence
(Misc2) (Ultimate) Positivity of Linear Recurrence
.

22.3
Computational Geometry

(CG-Open1) Geometric Thickness

Given:

A graph
G
, a natural number
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Question:

Does
G
have a straight-line drawing in which the edges can be colored with
k
colors in such a way that no crossing in the drawing involves two edges of the same color?

Complexity:

Known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard even for
k=2

[
DGM16
]
, but no better upper bound than
\exists\mathbb{R}
is known, including the case
k=2
. For multigraphs, and
k=30
the problem is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete
[
FKM+24
]
.

Comments:

Geometric thickness was introduced by Kainen
[
Kai73
]
. It has similarities to the simultaneous geometric embedding problem
(CG55) Simultaneous Geometric Embedding (SGE)
, which is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete. For a brief discussion, see
[
Sch21b
, Remark 7]
.

Also see:

Geometric thickness of multigraphs
(CG56) Geometric Thickness of Multigraph
, simultaneous geometric embedding
(CG55) Simultaneous Geometric Embedding (SGE)
.

(CG-Open2) Colin de Verdière Invariant

Given:

A graph
G=(V,E)
and a number
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Question:

Is the Colin de Verdière invariant
\mu(G)
at least
k
? For a definition see the comment section below.

Complexity:

Lies in
\exists\mathbb{R}
as shown by Kaluza and Tancer
[
KT19
]
.

Comments:

The graph parameter was introduced by Colin de Verdière
[
CdV90
, French]

and is connected to well-studied graph classes.
For example
\mu(G)\leq 2
if and only if
G
is an outerplanar graph and
\mu(G)\leq 3
if and only if
G
is a planar graph
[
vdHLS99
]
.
The parameter
\mu(G)
is the largest corank
of a symmetric matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that

–

ij\in E
implies
M_{ij}<0
and
M_{ij}=0
otherwise;

–

M
has exactly one negative eigenvalue, of multiplicity 1;

–

There is a matrix
X
such that
MX=0
and
X
has only zero
entries on the diagonal and at the non-zero entries of
M
.

For fixed
k
the invariant
\mu
can be computed in polynomial-time, since it is minor-closed.

Also see:

minimum graph rank
(A20) Minimum Graph Rank
, minimium matrix rank
(A16) Minimum/Maximum Matrix Rank
.

(CG-Open3) Unit Disk Contact Graph

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is
G
the
contact graph
of a collection of disjoint (open) unit disks in the plane, where two disks form a contact if they share exactly one point on their boundaries?

Complexity:

Known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard
[
BK96
,
KNP15
]
even if
G
is outerplanar.

Comments:

Unit disk contact graphs are planar, and by Koebe’s theorem all planar graphs are contact graphs of disks. There also is a weak version, which allows contacts between disks whose corresponding vertices are not adjacent; weak unit disk contact graphs thus are the subgraphs of unit disk contact graphs.

Also see:

Ply number
(CG-Open4) Ply Number
, kissing number
(CG-Open19) Kissing Number
.

(CG-Open4) Ply Number

Given:

A graph
G
, an integer
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Is there a straight-line drawing of
G
with the following property: If at each vertex we draw an open disk with radius half the length of the longest edge incident to the vertex, then no more than
k
disks intersect at any point in the plane? The smallest such
k
is the
ply number
of
G
.

Complexity:

The problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, since having ply number at most
1
is equivalent to being a unit disk contact graph
(CG-Open3) Unit Disk Contact Graph
, see
[
DGDH+15
]
, and that problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard
[
KNP15
]
.

Comments:

The ply number of a graph was introduced as a new metric for measuring the quality of graph drawings in
[
DGDH+15
]
.

Also see:

Unit distance graphs
(CG64) (Strict) Unit Distance Graph
, unit disk contact graphs
(CG-Open3) Unit Disk Contact Graph
.

(CG-Open5) Free Space Diagram Realizability

Given:

A
free space diagram
, that is, a rectangle
[0,\ell_{1}]\times[0,\ell_{2}]
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
which is subdivided into a grid of rectangular cells; each cell may be associated with an ellipse (which is cropped to the area of the cell), and a parameter
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.

Question:

Are there two polygonal curves
P_{1},P_{2}
of length
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
such that the union of the croppsed ellipses in the free space diagram corresponds to
\{(x,y):\lVert P_{1}(x)-P_{2}(y)\rVert_{2}\leq\varepsilon\}
? Here,
P(z)
denotes the parameterization of polygon
P
by length.

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
.

Comments:

The paper
[
ABM+23
]
shows that planar linkage realizability
(CG65) Linkage Realizability
reduces to free space diagram realizability, but an earlier claim that planar linkage realizability is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete may have been erroneous, leaving the complexity of the free space diagram realizability problem in limbo as well.

Also see:

Free space matrix realizability
(CG8) Free Space Matrix Realizability
, linkage realizability
(CG65) Linkage Realizability
, Fréchet distance of surfaces
(CG-Open29) Fréchet Distance of Surfaces
.

(CG-Open6) Maximum Rectilinear Crossing Number

Given:

A graph
G
, an integer
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Does
G
have a straight-line drawing with at least
k
crossings? The largest such
k
is the
maximum rectilinear crossing number
of
G
.

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
. Bald, Johnson, and Liu
[
BJL16
]
showed that the problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

Open Questions:

What is the complexity of the maximum rectilinear local crossing number?

Also see:

Rectilinear crossing number
(CG49) Rectilinear Crossing Number
, rectilinear local crossing number
(CG51) Rectilinear Local Crossing Number
.

(CG-Open7) Partial Drawing Extensibility

Given:

A graph
G
, prescribed locations for a subset of the vertices of
G
.

Question:

Is there a planar straight-line drawing of
G
respecting the prescribed vertex locations?

Complexity:

Known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard by Patrignani
[
Pat06
]
, in
\exists\mathbb{R}
by definition.

Comments:

Closely related to the graph in a polygonal domain problem
(CG47) Graph in a Polygonal Domain
. The graph on segments with obstacles problem
(CG48) Drawing Graph on Segments with Obstacles
can be considered a restricted variant.

Also see:

, graph on segments with obstacles

.

(CG-Open8) Geometric Partial Planarity

Given:

G=(V,E)
, a subset of edges
F\subseteq E
.

Question:

G
in which all edges in
F
are crossing-free?

Comments:

If we replace “are crossing-free” with “have at most one crossing”, then the problem is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, see geometric partial
1
-planarity

1

-Planarity
.

Also see:

1
-planarity

, rectilinear local crossing number

.

(CG-Open9) Convex Neural Codes

Given:

A
combinatorial neural code

\mathcal{C}
: a set of subsets of
[n]
.

Question:

Does there exist a collection of convex sets
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}
in
R^{d}
, for some fixed constant
d\geq 2
,
such that
\mathcal{C}=\{I(x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
, where
I(x)=\{i\in[n]:x\in X_{i}\}
is the
intersection pattern
of
x
?

Complexity:

\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard even for
d=2
, from
[
KLR23
]
, and decidable if
d=2
, from
[
BJ23
]
.

Open Questions:

Does the problem lie in
\exists\mathbb{R}
for
d=2
?
Is the problem decidable for
d=3
?

Also see:

, nerves of convex sets

, finite convex geometry realizability

(CG-Open10) Convex Clustered Planarity

Given:

A graph
G=(V,E)
, a hierarchy
{\cal C}\subseteq 2^{V}
. In a
hierarchy
if any two sets,
clusters
, intersect, one most be a subset of the other.

Question:

Is there a straight-line drawing of
G
such that for every
C\in{\cal C}
the convex hull of the vertices in
C
contains no vertices not in
C
, and any edge that crosses the boundary of the convex hull of
C
may do so at most once? This is a
clustered convex
straight-line embedding of
G
.

Comments:

This is a variant of the clustered planarity problem, which is now known to be solvable in
\mathsf{P}

[
FT22
]
. The traditional variant does not allow vertices on the boundary of a cluster. This may not be the most likely candidate for
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hardness, is there a stretchability result like Fáry’s theorem instead?

Also see:

Simultaneous geometric embedding
(CG55) Simultaneous Geometric Embedding (SGE)
, convex set intersection graph
(CG31) Convex Set Intersection Graph
.

(CG-Open11) Arc Number

Given:

A graph
G
, an integer
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Is there a crossing-free circular-arc drawing of
G
which is the union of
k
circular arcs? The smallest such
k
is known as the
arc number
.

Comments:

The arc number was introduced by Schulz
[
Sch15
]
as a variation of the segment number
(CG43) Segment Number
.

Also see:

Segment number
(CG43) Segment Number
, Lombardi drawability
(CG58) Lombardi-Drawability With Rotation System
, spherical cover number

.

(CG-Open12) Spherical Cover Number

Given:

A graph
G
, an integer
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Are there
k
spheres in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
whose union contains a crossing-free circular-arc drawing of
G
? We say the spheres
cover

G
.

Complexity:

Lies in
\exists\mathbb{R}
.

Comments:

Introduced by Kryven, Ravsky, and Wolff
[
KRW19
]
, the case
d=2
is also known as the
circle cover number
.

(CG-Open14) Upward Outerplanar Slope Number

Given:

A directed graph
G
, an integer
k\geq 0
. A (straight-line) drawing of
G
is
upward
if
u
lies below
v
for every directed edge
uv\in E(G)
.

Question:

Is there an upward, outerplanar straight-line drawing of
G
in which the edges are drawn using at most
k
different slopes?

Complexity:

\mathsf{NP}
-hard by Klawitter and Zink
[
KZ23
]
for
k=3
.

Comments:

A drawing realizing the planar slope number of the graph may not be possible on a grid, and it is
\exists\mathbb{Q}
-complete to decide whether a grid realization exists, and there is a
2^{2^{\Omega(n^{1/3})}}
lower bound on the grid size
[
Hof17
]
.

Open Questions:

How hard is it to determine the (non-planar) slope number of a graph
[
Hof17
]
?

Also see:

Planar slope number
(CG40) Planar Slope Number
, upward planar slope number
(CG41) Upward Planar Slope Number
.

(CG-Open15) Incidence Graph of Geometric Configuration

Given:

A bipartite cubic graph
G=(L\cup P,E)
.

Question:

Is
G
the incidence (Levi) graph of a geometric
3
-configuration, that is, are there points for
P
, and lines for
L
such that
E
has an edge
p\ell
if and only if
p
is incident to
\ell
?

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
.

Comments:

Asked by Pisanski on mathoverflow
[
Pis
]
. This is a special case of the linear hypergraph realizability problem
(CG34) Linear Hypergraph Realizability
.

Also see:

Segment intersection graph
(CG25) Segment Intersection Graph (SEG)
, pseudo-segment stretchability

, linear hypergraph realizability

.

(CG-Open16) Representing Graph with Orthogonal Vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

Given:

A graph
G
on
n
vertices.

Question:

Can we assign
n
distinct vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
to the vertices in
G
so that any two vectors belonging to adjacent vertices are orthogonal to each other? (No restrictions on non-adjacent vertices.)

Complexity:

Arends, Ouaknine, Joël and Wampler
[
AOW11
]
show that the problem lies in
\exists\mathbb{R}
.

Comments:

Arends, Ouaknine, Joël and Wampler name this problem the “embeddability problem”. They arrive at it in their search for small Kochen-Specker vector systems, which play a role in quantum computing. They consider additional restrictions on
G
which are natural in this context, but which are all of an
\mathsf{NP}
nature, so the problem, as presented here seems to capture the computational core of the problem.

Also see:

Dot-product graph
(CG24) Dot-Product Graph in

R
d

, unit ball graph
(CG23) Unit Ball Graph in

R
d

.

(CG-Open17) Graph Flattenability

Given:

A graph
G
, integer
d\geq 0
.

Question:

For every straight-line embedding of
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
, with arbitrary
d^{\prime}\geq d
, is there a straight-line embedding in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the same distances between all pairs of vertices?

Complexity:

The problem lies in
\forall\exists\mathbb{R}

[
BC07
]
(the authors observe that it is equivalent to testing the equality of two semialgebraic sets). For fixed
d
, graph flattenability is minor-closed and can therefore be solved in polynomial time; for hardness results it will therefore be essential that
d
is part of the input. Lists of excluded minors are known for
d\leq 3

[
BC07
]
.

Comments:

The problem was introduced under the name
d
-realizability by Belk and Connelly
[
BC07
]
, but it had been studied earlier.

Open Questions:

Is the problem
\mathsf{NP}
- or
\mathsf{coNP}
-hard?

Also see:

Graph realizability
(CG62) Graph Realizability
, area universality
(CG-Open33) Area Universality
, unit distance graph
(CG64) (Strict) Unit Distance Graph
.

(CG-Open18) Faithful Drawability of Edge-Colored Graph

Given:

A graph
G
and a proper edge-coloring of
G
, that is, any two adjacent edges differ in color.

Question:

Is there a
faithful
drawing of
G
, that is, a drawing of
G
in which all edges of the same color lie on concurrent lines, and no two edges lie on the same line?

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
, but otherwise open even for
4
-regular graphs.

Comments:

Richter shows that faithful drawability can be recognized in polynomial time for cubic graphs and conjectures that the problem is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete for
4
=regular graphs
[
Ric11
, Conjecture 9]
. By moving the common points of the bundles of concurrent lines to infinity, we obtain a drawing in which all edges of the same color are parallel; this seems to suggest a connection to the planar slope number
(CG40) Planar Slope Number
.

Also see:

Planar slope number
(CG40) Planar Slope Number
.

(CG-Open19) Kissing Number

Given:

A compact semialgebraic set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, an integer
k\geq 0
..

Question:

Are there
k
non-overlapping congruent copies of
S
each touching
S
? The largest such
k
is the
kissing number
of
S
.

Complexity:

Open.

Comments:

For many natural geometric sets, such as polygons or spheres, there are known upper bounds on the kissing number, which places the problem in
\exists\mathbb{R}
, so solvers can, in principle, be used to find the kissing number in those instances. A similar problem, the largest number of pairwise touching non-overlapping congruent copies of cylinders, was tackled using a similar, but more sophisticated approach, see
[
BLR15
]
.

Also see:

Unit disk contact graph
(CG-Open3) Unit Disk Contact Graph
.

(CG-Open20) Euclidean Minimum Spanning Tree

Given:

A tree
T
.

Question:

Is
T
the Euclidean minimum spanning tree of a set of points in the plane? In the Euclidean minimum spanning tree the weights of the edges are the distances between the points in the plane.

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
and known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard by Eades and Whitesides
[
EW96a
]
.

Comments:

The maximum degree of
T
has to be
6
, since every tree of maximum degree
5
is a Euclidean Minimum Spanning Tree; the construction showing this requires a double-exponential grid; as far as we know it is open whether this can be improved
[
MS92
]
.

Also see:

Nearest neighbor graph
(CG-Open21) Nearest Neighbor Graph
, Gabriel graph
(CG-Open22) Gabriel Graph
.

(CG-Open21) Nearest Neighbor Graph

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is
G
the nearest neighbor graph of a set of points in the plane? The
nearest-neighbor graph
of a point set has a vertex for each point, and there is an edge between two vertices if they are nearest neighbors to each other. Here
p
is a
nearest neighbor
of
q
if there is no point closer to
p
than
q
.

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
and known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard by Eades and Whitesides
[
EW96b
]
.

Comments:

There are several variants of this problem, the variant we presented here is called the
mutual
nearest neighbor graph in
[
EW96b
]
.

Also see:

Euclidean minimum spanning tree
(CG-Open20) Euclidean Minimum Spanning Tree
, Gabriel graph
(CG-Open22) Gabriel Graph
.

(CG-Open22) Gabriel Graph

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is
G
a Gabriel graph in the plane? The
Gabriel graph
of a point set has a vertex for each point, and there is an edge between two vertices
pq
if the disk with diameter
pq
contains no other vertices of the graph.

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
and known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard by Eades and Whitesides
[
EW96b
]
.

Comments:

The realization of a Gabriel graph contains the Euclidean minimum spanning tree
(CG-Open20) Euclidean Minimum Spanning Tree
and the nearest neighborhood graph
(CG-Open21) Nearest Neighbor Graph
of the point set, and is itself a subset of the Delaunay triangulation
(CG72) Delaunay Triangulation
.

Also see:

Euclidean minimum spanning tree
(CG-Open20) Euclidean Minimum Spanning Tree
, nearest neighbor graph
(CG-Open21) Nearest Neighbor Graph
, Delaunay triangulation
(CG72) Delaunay Triangulation
.

(CG-Open23) Hidden Set in Polygon

Given:

A simple polygon
P
, natural number
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Are there
k
points inside the region enclosed by
P
which cannot pairwise see each other?

Complexity:

Known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard by Eidenbenz
[
Eid02
]
and to lie in
\exists\mathbb{R}

[
Fan20
]
.

Comments:

As with visibility graphs one can consider variants in which the hidden points must lie on the boundary or the polygon has holes. There are also variants based on terrains rather than polygons. (This problem is also sometimes called the
bird nesting box problem
. The idea is that you want to place as many bird nesting boxes as possible. However, if two bird nesting boxes see each other they may be avoided by birds as many bird species avoid other birds.)

Also see:

Visibility graphs
(CG35) (Point) Visibility Graph
.

(CG-Open24) Watchman Route Problem

Given:

A polygon with holes, an integer
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Is there a polygonal curve of length at most
k
such that every point of the polygon can be seen from some point on the polygonal curve?

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
, as shown by Volk
[
Vol23
]
.

\mathsf{NP}
-hardness is shown by Dumitrescu and Tóth
[
DT12
]
, who explain why the earlier
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness proof by Chin and Ntafos
[
CN88
]
, in the paper that introduced the problem, does not work. The problem is polynomial-time solvable for simple polygons, see
[
DT12
]
for more background.

Comments:

The definition of the problem only gives
\exists\forall\mathbb{R}
-membership, showing the problem lies in
\exists\mathbb{R}
requires additional analysis. Volk
[
Vol23
]
also considers the variant in which the polygonal curve has at most
k
bends.

Open Questions:

What is the complexity of the problem in unbounded dimension? In that case,
\exists\forall\mathbb{R}
is the best upper bound.

Also see:

Lawn moving problem
(CG-Open25) Lawn Mowing Problem
art gallery problem
(CG16) Art Gallery Problem
, visibility graph of polygon with holes
(CG36) Visibility Graph of Polygon with Holes
.

(CG-Open25) Lawn Mowing Problem

Given:

A polygon with holes, an integer
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Is there a polygonal curve of length at most
k
such that every point of the polygon has distance at most
1
from the polygonal curve?

Complexity:

In
\exists\forall\mathbb{R}
, as shown by Volk
[
Vol23
]
and
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, as shown by Arkin, Fekete, and Mitchell
[
AFM00
]
.

Comments:

As an intermediate problem, Volk
[
Vol23
]
works with the variant in which the polygonal curve has at most
k
bends. Arkin, Fekete, and Mitchell
[
AFM00
]
also showed
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of the
milling problem
, in which the mower may not cross the boundary of the polygon with holes.

Open Questions:

Does the problem lie in
\exists\mathbb{R}
? For the similar

Also see:

Watchman route problem
(CG-Open24) Watchman Route Problem
, art gallery problem
(CG16) Art Gallery Problem
.

(CG-Open26) Sweeping Points

Given:

Finite set
P\subseteq Q^{2}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.

Question:

Can all the points be swept to the origin using sweeps of total length at most
\ell
? In a single
sweep
, an infinite line is moved in parallel from one location to another location (the distance swept is the contribution of that sweep to the total length). Points encountered along the sweep get moved with the line.

Comments:

Dumitrescu and Jiang
[
DJ11
]
attribute this problem to Paweł Żyliński; they study approximation algorithms, and ask whether it can be solved in polynomial time.

Open Questions:

Can the shortest length solution always be achieved in a polynomial number of sweeps
[
DJ11
]
?

(CG-Open27) Hypercube

Given:

Finite set of points
P\subseteq[0,1]^{d}
, a natural number
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Question:

Is there a pointset
Q\subseteq[0,1]^{d}
of
size
k
such that
P\subset\conv(Q)
, where
\conv(Q)
is the convex hull of
Q
?

Complexity:

The hypercube problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard via a reduction from 3SAT, and lies in
\exists\mathbb{R}
as shown by Kiefer and Wacther
[
KW14
]
. Kiefer and Wachter also observe that for fixed
d
, the problem lies in
\mathsf{P}
.

Comments:

The problem remains
\mathsf{NP}
-hard if
P
is required to contain the origin, the
restricted hypercube problem
. Kiefer and Wachter introduced this problem to isolate the geometric difficulty at the root of the
probabilitic automaton minimisation problem
(ML-Open1) Probabilistic Automaton Minimisation
.
The hypercube problem is reminiscent of the nested polytope problem
(CG71) Nested Polytopes
as defined by
[
DHM19
]
.

Open Questions:

Kiefer and Wachter
[
KW14
]
observe that for
d=2
the set
Q
can always be assumed to be rational, and ask whether this remains true for higher dimensions.

Also see:

Probabilitic automaton minimisation
(ML-Open1) Probabilistic Automaton Minimisation
, nested polytope problem
(CG71) Nested Polytopes
.

(CG-Open28)
k
-Median of Polygonal Curves

Given:

A family of polygonal curves
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, all of the same length,
c\in\mathbb{Q}
.

Question:

Is there a polygonal curve
P
of length
k
such that
\sum_{i\in[n]}d_{F}(P,P_{i})\leq c
? Here,
d_{F}
denotes the Fréchet distance, see references for a definition.

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
, as shown by Rohde
[
Roh22
]
, and
\mathsf{NP}
-hard by Buchin, Driemel, and Struijs
[
BDS20
]
.

Comments:

The Fréchet distance between two polygonal curves can be computed efficiently
[
AG92
]
.

Also see:

Fréchet distance of surfaces
(CG-Open29) Fréchet Distance of Surfaces
.

(CG-Open29) Fréchet Distance of Surfaces

Given:

Two homeomorphic surfaces
R
and
S
together with immersions
\varphi_{R}:R\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and
\varphi_{S}:S\Rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\delta\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.

Question:

Is the Fréchet distance
\delta_{F}(R,S)\leq\delta
? Here
\delta_{F}(R,S)
is the infimum over all homeomorphisms
f:R\rightarrow S
of
\sup_{x\in R}\lVert\varphi_{R}(x)-\varphi_{S}(f(x))\rVert_{2}
.

Complexity:

One has to assume that
R
,
S
,
\varphi_{R}
and
\varphi_{S}
have reasonable effective representations for this problem to fall into the domain of
\exists\mathbb{R}
. There are various relevant results, e.g. Godau’s proof that the problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard
[
God99
]
, and Nayyeri and Xu’s result that the problem lies in
\exists\mathbb{R}
if one of the surfaces is a triangle
[
NX18
]
.

Also see:

Hausdorff distance
(A43) Hausdorff Distance
,
k
-Median of Polygonal Curves
(CG-Open28)

k

-Median of Polygonal Curves
.

(CG-Open30) Compatible Triangulations of Polygons with Holes

Given:

Two vertex-labeled polygons
P
and
Q
with holes, an integer
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Can
P
and
Q
be extended to vertex-labeled triangulations which are compatible by adding at most
k
points? Here two triangulations are
compatible
if they have the same rotation system, that is, the cyclic permutations of neighbors at each vertex is the same for both triangulations.

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
and
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, as shown by Lubiw and Mondal
[
LM20
]
, who also ask whether the problem is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-hard.

Comments:

The additional points are known as
Steiner points
; the given polygons have to be compatible to start with for the problem to be solvable.

Open Questions:

Lubiw and Mondal
[
LM20
]
ask whether the problem remains
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for simple polygons (no holes).

(CG-Open31) Convexity Through Translation

Given:

A family of point sets in
\mathbb{Q}^{2}
.

Question:

Can the point sets be translated so that the resulting points are in convex position, that is, lie on the convex hull of the union of the translated point sets?

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
and
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, as shown by Hoffmann, Kusters, Rote, Saumell, and Silveira
[
HKR+13
]
.

Comments:

If the point sets can be scaled as well as translated, the problem is not even known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard
[
HKR+13
]
.

Also see:

Convexity of semialgebraic set
(A32) Convexity of Semialgebraic Set
.

(CG-Open32) Prescribed Area

Given:

A plane graph
G
, an area assignment for
G
, where an
area assignment
for
G
assigns a positive real number to every face of
G
.

Question:

Does
G
have a straight-line drawing realizing the area assignment, that is, each face having the area prescribed by the area assignment?

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
. If some of the vertex locations can be fixed, the problem is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, as shown by Dobbins, Kleist, Miltzow and Rzążewski
[
DKMR23
]
.

Comments:

Dobbins, Kleist, Miltzow and Rzążewski
[
DKMR23
]
conjecture that this problem is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete.

Also see:

Prescribed area extension
(CG60) Prescribed Area Extension
, area universality
(CG-Open33) Area Universality
, area universality for triples with partial area assignment
(CG75) Area Universality For Triples with Partial Area Assignment
.

(CG-Open33) Area Universality

Given:

A plane graph
G
.

Question:

An
area assignment
for
G
assigns a positive real number to every face of
G
. Can every area assignment be realized by a straight-line embedding of
G
? In that realization, every face must have the area assigned to it.

Complexity:

Dobbins, Kleist, and Miltzow show that the problem lies in
\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
and conjecture that it is
\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete. They can show that a more restricted version, area universality for triples with partial area assignments
(CG75) Area Universality For Triples with Partial Area Assignment
, is
\forall\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete.

Also see:

Area Universality for triples with partial area assignments
(CG75) Area Universality For Triples with Partial Area Assignment
, prescribed area
(CG-Open32) Prescribed Area
.

(CG-Open34) Segment Visibility Graph

Given:

A graph
G
.

Question:

Is
G
the visibility graph of a set of segments in the plane? The visibility graph of a set of segments represents each segment by a vertex, and there is an edge between two vertices if the corresponding segments can see each other, that is, if there is a line-segment connecting the two segments that does not intersect any other segment.

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
.

Comments:

There is also the endpoint segment visibility graph, which is the visibility graph of the endpoints of the segments, but the full segments are still the visibility obstacles.

Also see:

Point visibility graph
(CG35) (Point) Visibility Graph
, visibility graph of polygon with holes
(CG36) Visibility Graph of Polygon with Holes
,
internal/external polygon visibility graph
(CG37) Internal/External Polygon Visibility Graph
, obstacle number
(CG-Open35) Obstacle Number
.

(CG-Open35) Obstacle Number

Given:

A graph
G
, an integer
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Is
G
the visibility graph of a set of points (in general position) in the plane with at most
k
polygonal obstacles? Here two points can
see
each other if the straight-line segment connecting them does not intersect any obstacles.

Complexity:

In
\exists\mathbb{R}
. This problem is not known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, though various variants and restrictions of it are, see the summary in
[
GOdMV18
]
, and
[
BCG+24
]
, which shows that the problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for a single, fixed obstacle.

Comments:

Obstacle numbers were introduced by Alpert, Koch and Liason
[
AKL10
]
. There also is a variant known as the
planar obstacle number
in which the visibility segments may not cross each other
[
GOdMV18
]
.

Also see:

(Point) visibility graph
(CG35) (Point) Visibility Graph
, segment visibility graph
(CG-Open34) Segment Visibility Graph
.

(CG-Open36) Fiber Dimension

Given:

A graph
G
, a natural number
d\in\mathbb{N}
.

Question:

Does
G
have fiber dimension at most
d
? That is, is there a polytope
P\subseteq\mathbb{Q}^{d}
and a symmetric set
M\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
G
is isomorphic to the graph on
V=P\cap\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with an edge
uv
if and only if
u-v\in M
.

Complexity:

We are not aware of any computational complexity results.

Comments:

The fiber dimension of a graph was defined by Windisch
[
Win19
]
. Every graph has finite fiber dimension.

(CG-Open37) Treetope graph

Given:

A graph
G
, an integer
k\geq 0
.

Question:

Is there a
k
-treetope polytope
P
with
1
-skeleton
G
? A
k
-treetope

is a
k
-dimensional polytope with a special base facet
F
that intersects every face of dimension at least two in at least two points.

Complexity:

In dimension four Eppstein showed that the question is polynomial time solvable
[
Epp20
]
.
The complexity of this problem is open for dimension five and above.

Comments:

Eppstein states: “
Recognizing the graphs of pyramids over arbitrary 4-polytopes, and therefore also
recognizing the graphs of 5-treetopes, is as difficult as recognizing the graphs of
arbitrary 4-polytopes, which we expect to be complete for the existential theory of the
reals

[
Epp20
]
.”

Also see:

Polytope.

22.4
Game Theory

(GT-Open1) Recursive Markov Chain Acceptance Probability

Given:

A recursive Markov chain with associated monotone transition operator
P:\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\geq 0}
and a probability vector
c\in\mathbb{Q}^{b}
(for definitions, see
[
EY09
]
).

Question:

Is there a fixed point of
P
below
c
, that is an
x
with
P(x)=x
and
x\leq c
(in all coordinates)?

Complexity:

Etessami and Yannakakis
[
EY09
]
show that this (and some related problems) can be expressed in
\exists\mathbb{R}
, and is at least as hard as the sum of square root problem.

Also see:

Büchi automaton accepting recursive Markov chain trajectory
(GT-Open2) Büchi Automaton Accepting Recursive Markov Chain Trajectory

(GT-Open2) Büchi Automaton Accepting Recursive Markov Chain Trajectory

Given:

A Büchi automaton
\mathcal{A}
and a recursive Markov chain
\mathcal{M}
(for definitions, see
[
BLW13b
]
).

Question:

Is there a trajectory of
\mathcal{M}
which is accepted by
\mathcal{A}
?

Complexity:

Building on work by Etessami and Yannakakis; Benedikt, Lenhardt, and Worrell
[
BLW13b
]
show that the problem lies in
\exists\mathbb{R}
.

Also see:

Recursive Markov chain acceptance probability
(GT-Open1) Recursive Markov Chain Acceptance Probability

22.5
Machine Learning and Probabilistic Reasoning

(ML-Open1) Probabilistic Automaton Minimisation

Given:

A probabilistic automaton
A
, a natural number
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Question:

Does there exists a probabilistic automaton
A^{\prime}
of size
n
so that
A
and
A^{\prime}
are
equivalent?

Complexity:

The problem was shown to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard by Kiefer and Wachter
[
KW14
]

via a reduction from the restricted hypercube problem
(CG-Open27) Hypercube
, which is known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard
[
KW14
]
. The problem is known to be contained in
\exists\mathbb{R}

[
KW14
]
.

Comments:

The restricted hypercube problem is similar to the known
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete nested polytope problem
(CG71) Nested Polytopes
, so there is hope to strengthen the result to
\exists\mathbb{R}
-completeness.

Also see:

Hypercube problem
(CG-Open27) Hypercube
.

22.6
Markov Chains and Decision Processes

(MDP-Open1) Universal Safety for Probabilistic Finite State Automata

Given:

A probabilistic finite state automata
\mathcal{A}

[
AGV18
]
and a safety polytope
H
given by its vertex representation.

Question:

Is it true that for every starting distribution there exists a strategy such that
\mathcal{A}
stays in the safety polytope
H
?

Complexity:

Akshay, Genest, and Vyas show that the problem lies in
\exists\mathbb{R}

[
AGV18
]
.

Comments:

The problem has many variants. One can ask for existential safety instead of universal safety, replace probabilistic finite state automata with Markov Decision Processes, or encode the safety polytope in different ways. None of these variants are known to be
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete either
[
AGV18
]
.
